**Proposition-level authority:** Stone’s opinion, joined by the other eight Justices; unanimous holding on both authorization under the Rules and the Article III objection.

**Current force:** Remains controlling as stated for the then-effective Rule 11 and the independent procedural violation adjudicated.

**Material application:** Rules 1 and 81(c) govern civil proceedings after removal, and Rule 11 supplies the particular sanctioning authority. The completed misconduct was misuse of the court’s procedure, not Willy’s successful jurisdictional objection. The propriety of the violation, fee response and joint liability was accepted on this limited review.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No hypothetical merits jurisdiction, general inherent sanctioning power or universal collateral-order exception is recognized. A different order requires its own lawful source and genuine independence. The accepted misconduct and amount are not reconsidered.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the $19,307 sanction, jointly and severally against Willy and his attorney, without enlargement. The prior jurisdictional direction returning the employment claims to state court remains operative; the invalid federal merits judgment is not revived.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Sibbach v. Wilson & Co.*, 312 U.S. 1, preserves the prohibition on procedural rules enlarging substantive rights or jurisdiction. *Cooter & Gell v. Hartmarx Corp.*, 496 U.S. 384, is extended from voluntary dismissal to this erroneous assertion of merits jurisdiction because Rule 11 adjudication is collateral. *United States v. Mine Workers*, 330 U.S. 258, supports orderly determination of disputed authority without assumed merits power. *United States Catholic Conference v. Abortion Rights Mobilization, Inc.*, 487 U.S. 72, is distinguished: coercive discovery serving unauthorized merits adjudication lacks the independence of this completed sanction.

### Diversity jurisdiction and domestic-relations abstention

#### Ankenbrandt v. Richards, 504 U.S. 689 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-367.
**Decided:** June 15, 1992.

##### Whether Article III or the domestic-relations exception to §1332 excludes this diverse tort damages action

**Holding:** Article III does not exclude domestic-relations disputes from the federal judicial power. Section 1332 carries a settled domestic-relations exception limited to issuance of divorce, alimony, and child-custody decrees; that exception does not exclude a diverse parties’ tort action seeking damages for alleged child abuse without such a decree.

**Proposition-level authority:** White's opinion of the Court, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter; five Justices adopt this proposition. The judgment reversing dismissal is unanimous, but the separate judgment concurrences do not add joins to the Court's foundational statutory holding.

**Material application:** The complaint seeks compensation from the children's father and his companion for alleged abuse, without requesting a divorce, alimony, custody decree or review of a state decree. The settled statutory understanding survives the 1948 recodification; Article III itself contains no domestic-relations exclusion.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The exception concerns issuance of divorce, alimony and child-custody decrees, not every dispute among relatives. No federal tort is created; liability, parental immunity, damages, the alleged abuse and any custody or support decree are not adjudicated.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Fifth Circuit judgment is reversed and the complaint remanded for ordinary proceedings and defenses. The Court issues or modifies no domestic decree and awards no damages.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Barber v. Barber, 62 U.S. (21 How.) 582 (1859): retained as the basis for the limited decree exception and distinguished from a prohibition of ordinary damages actions.
- De la Rama v. De la Rama, 201 U.S. 303 (1906): its distinction between territorial authority and ordinary decree jurisdiction remains; it does not exclude this tort action.
- In re Burrus, 136 U.S. 586 (1890): broad domestic-relations language does not enlarge the exception to this diverse damages suit.
- Patterson v. McLean Credit Union, 491 U.S. 164 (1989): statutory stare decisis supports preservation of the settled limited construction; no separate civil-rights issue is decided.
- Fourco Glass Co. v. Transmirra Products Corp., 353 U.S. 222 (1957): a recodification is not presumed to alter substantive law absent a clear indication.
- Lorillard v. Pons, 434 U.S. 575 (1978): congressional retention of language against an established judicial construction supports the retained limited exception.

##### Whether Younger abstention supports dismissal without an ongoing state proceeding

**Holding:** Younger abstention cannot sustain this dismissal because there is no ongoing state proceeding with which the federal damages action would interfere.

**Proposition-level authority:** White's opinion of the Court, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter; five Justices adopt this proposition. The judgment reversing dismissal is unanimous, but the separate judgment concurrences do not add joins to the Court's foundational statutory holding.

**Material application:** The action seeks compensation for past misconduct and does not halt, supervise or displace an ongoing state adjudication. No state proceeding is alleged to be pending.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court leaves Younger's outer range in other domestic-relations controversies undecided. A family setting or the possibility of a future state application supplies no ongoing proceeding.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Younger cannot sustain the dismissal; ordinary proceedings on the damages complaint must resume, subject to the separately stated limits.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Younger v. Harris, 401 U.S. 37 (1971): retained; its ongoing-proceeding premise is absent here.
- New Orleans Public Service, Inc. v. Council of New Orleans, 491 U.S. 350 (1989): applied to require a concrete abstention basis rather than general reluctance to exercise federal jurisdiction.

##### Whether Burford abstention supports dismissal without unsettled domestic status or relevant state-policy interference

**Holding:** Burford abstention does not authorize dismissal of this ordinary diversity tort action where adjudication neither requires resolution of an unsettled domestic status nor threatens the state-policy interference that supports that form of abstention.

**Proposition-level authority:** White's opinion of the Court, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter; five Justices adopt this proposition. The judgment reversing dismissal is unanimous, but the separate judgment concurrences do not add joins to the Court's foundational statutory holding.

**Material application:** Family status has already been determined and its present legal character does not decide the alleged torts or compensation. State expertise and an important family-policy interest alone cannot replace the absent interference.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Materially different future circumstances remain open. An unresolved status issue necessary to the federal action together with threatened interference in the relevant state process would present a different question; that hypothetical is not adjudicated here.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Burford cannot sustain this dismissal; the reinstated tort complaint returns for ordinary proceedings and defenses.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Burford v. Sun Oil Co., 319 U.S. 315 (1943): retained within its state-policy and administrative-coherence rationale; no basis for abstention is shown here.
- New Orleans Public Service, Inc. v. Council of New Orleans, 491 U.S. 350 (1989): applied to confine Burford abstention to concrete interference, not the mere importance of state interests.

### Extraordinary writs

#### In re Blodgett, 502 U.S. 236 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-716.
**Decided:** January 13, 1992.

##### Whether mandamus is available before focused relief is sought from the court that entered the operative abeyance order

**Holding:** Mandamus is unavailable where the petitioner has not first sought focused relief from the court that entered the operative abeyance order and has not shown that avenue inadequate or futile. Denial may be without prejudice to a renewed petition after the ordinary avenue is pursued.

**Proposition-level authority:** The per curiam opinion for Stone, White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Thomas, together with Stevens's concurrence joined by Blackmun, expressly states this dispositive prerequisite for all nine Justices. This is an actual majority-supported threshold proposition across writings.

**Material application:** Washington did not ask the Ninth Circuit panel to modify the August 1991 order awaiting related proceedings. Earlier general inquiries and a resubmission request did not establish that focused relief was unavailable or inevitably futile.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The decision does not approve every period of delay, find abuse of the writ, resolve Campbell's sentence, dissolve the stay, or impose an automatic special burden on future capital litigants. It creates no habeas-merits rule.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Mandamus is denied without prejudice to renewed application after the ordinary avenue is pursued; no habeas result or execution schedule is directed.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Kerr, Will, and Fahey are applied with Rule 20.1: mandamus requires an extraordinary failure to act, no adequate alternative avenue, and a clear and indisputable entitlement. Barefoot, Rose, and McCleskey are not extended.

#### In re Maria Luisa Costell Gaydos

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-5831.

**Decided:** 1996-12-02.

For financial leave/current-paper review and prospective fee-and-form conditions in In re Maria Luisa Costell Gaydos, No. 96-5831, decided 1996-12-02, see Federal Courts — Fee relief and prospective filing controls.

##### Extraordinary and original relief

**Holding:** The requested writ and alternative original action are denied: completed docketing cannot be compelled again, and this record establishes neither a proper original-jurisdiction category nor a clear and indisputable entitlement to the remaining commands with no adequate ordinary avenue. The denial leaves ordinary clarification or correction of the distinct certiorari submission available under the existing Rules without determining its timeliness, adequacy or ultimate treatment.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of the per curiam opinion, joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; eight Justices adopt the reasons. The Chief Justice agrees with this disposition without joining Part II.

**Material application:** The District Clerk docketed the FOIA complaint on September 9, and the complaint was dismissed the following week. Mandamus cannot compel the performed act or replace an appeal from the dismissal. A personal FOIA suit supplies no basis for this Court's original jurisdiction, and general accusations against clerks establish neither concrete disqualification nor a right to summonses here. The supplement's distinct access contention does not establish a right to require a new lower-court opinion. Rules 14.1(i) and 20.3 call for existing entered orders, opinions and essential material; they do not direct a lower court to write another opinion. The existing reasoned letter and an explanation that no additional opinion exists may be tendered through ordinary filing procedures. Blodgett applies Rule 20's requirement that adequate ordinary avenues be unavailable: this record does not establish that clarification of the stated deficiency or appropriate lower-court relief is unavailable or inadequate. Arguable access concerns therefore support financial leave without satisfying the more demanding prerequisites for extraordinary relief.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No new filing is commanded, no acceptance order issues, no correction is declared untimely, and no new deadline or default is imposed. The Court decides no FOIA merits, clerk misconduct or separate certiorari outcome.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The petition for extraordinary relief and the alternative original action are denied.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- In re Blodgett (January 13, 1992): applies the adequate-ordinary-avenue prerequisite to extraordinary relief without deciding the underlying merits.

### Federal corporate charters and removal

#### American National Red Cross v. S. G., 505 U.S. 247 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-594.
**Decided:** June 19, 1992.

##### Whether the Red Cross charter's State-or-Federal court clause confers original federal jurisdiction

**Holding:** The Red Cross charter’s authorization to sue and be sued in courts of law and equity, State or Federal, confers original federal jurisdiction over actions to which the Red Cross is a party. In construing a congressional charter’s sue-and-be-sued clause, a specific mention of federal courts permits a jurisdictional reading that general corporate-capacity language does not; the charter grant here operates independently of federal incorporation alone.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, joined by White, Blackmun, Stevens and Thomas; five of nine participating Justices adopt the charter construction at this level of generality. Stone-Zsela agrees on his separate charter-specific ground but supplies no formal join.

**Material application:** Congress added the explicit federal-forum reference in 1947 against the charter precedents. The clause confers both capacity and jurisdiction and preserves concurrent state-court access. Section 1349's restriction on incorporation alone does not erase this specific additional grant.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Materially different charter wording and §1349's application to nonstock corporations remain unresolved. Jurisdiction creates no substantive federal tort rule, liability or immunity.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The First Circuit's contrary charter ruling is reversed; the special grant supports the removal holding below.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Bank of the United States v. Deveaux, 9 U.S. 61: distinguished; its general-capacity clause remains insufficient to grant federal jurisdiction.
- Osborn v. Bank of the United States, 22 U.S. 738: applied for the jurisdictional effect of express federal-forum language.
- Bankers Trust Co. v. Texas & Pacific Railway, 241 U.S. 295: distinguished; its generalized court language remains a capacity provision.
- D’Oench, Duhme & Co. v. FDIC, 315 U.S. 447: applied for its stated jurisdictional use of materially comparable State-or-Federal language; no FDIC substantive rule is imported.
- Cannon v. University of Chicago, 441 U.S. 677: applied for Congress’s use of language with an established judicial meaning.
- United States v. Merriam, 263 U.S. 179: applied for interpreting statutory words against their established judicial meaning.

##### Whether the special charter grant permits §1441 removal of a complaint asserting state-law torts

**Holding:** Because the charter gives original federal jurisdiction over this Red Cross action, §1441 permits its removal even though the complaint alleges state-law torts. The well-pleaded-complaint limitation on statutory arising-under jurisdiction under §1331 does not eliminate this separate charter grant.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, joined by White, Blackmun, Stevens and Thomas; the same five Justices adopt this necessary answer to the alternative removal objection.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Diversity, joinder and independently available abstention retain their own requirements and remain unresolved. No tort element, defense, immunity or substantive-law issue is decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Removal is sustained on the charter ground and the case is remanded through the First Circuit for federal proceedings consistent with that grant. The Court neither requires nor forbids joinder and does not establish diversity.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Verlinden B. V. v. Central Bank of Nigeria, 461 U.S. 480: applied to distinguish §1331’s statutory arising-under limits from a separate original-jurisdiction grant.
- Osborn v. Bank of the United States, 22 U.S. 738: the special charter basis remains distinct from the substantive law governing the controversy.
- D’Oench, Duhme & Co. v. FDIC, 315 U.S. 447: its charter jurisdiction is not merged into the substantive law governing a particular controversy.

##### Whether Article III permits the Red Cross corporate-party jurisdiction granted by Congress

**Holding:** Article III’s arising-under judicial power permits Congress to confer original federal jurisdiction over actions involving a federally chartered corporation such as the Red Cross, although the immediate claim arises under state law. The charter’s specific jurisdictional grant therefore lies within Congress’s constitutional authority.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, joined by White, Blackmun, Stevens and Thomas; five Justices decide the constitutional objection. The dissent does not reach it, and Stone-Zsela supplies only his separate ground.

**Material application:** Federal law supplies the corporation's legal existence and powers; state-law issues do not remove that federal foundation. Article III's capacity is distinct from Congress's narrower choices in general jurisdiction statutes.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court applies the established charter principle without reconsidering its outer boundaries. Substantive duties, liability, defenses and relief remain undecided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The constitutional objection does not defeat this charter grant or its removal consequence.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Osborn v. Bank of the United States, 22 U.S. 738: applied and retained as the constitutional basis for corporate-party federal jurisdiction.
- Bankers Trust Co. v. Texas & Pacific Railway, 241 U.S. 295: its constitutional account remains distinct from its restrictive statutory result.
- Verlinden B. V. v. Central Bank of Nigeria, 461 U.S. 480: applied for the distinction between Article III judicial power and §1331’s statutory scope.

### Fee-waived filings

#### Zatko v. California, 502 U.S. 16 (1991)

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 91-5052, 91-5111, 91-5166, 91-5167, 91-5244, 91-5246, 91-5307, 91-5331, 91-5332, 91-5401, 91-5416, 91-5476, 91-5583, 91-5594, 91-5692, 91-5730, and 91-5732.  
**Decided:** November 4, 1991.

##### Whether extreme repetitive frivolous filing permits denial of pending Rule 39.8 fee-waiver motions

**Holding:** When the Court is satisfied that pending submissions are frivolous and the applicants' repeated frivolous filings constitute extreme abuse of its process, Rule 39.8 permits denial of in-forma-pauperis status, meaning permission to file without paying fees. Denial may require payment of the docketing fee and compliance with ordinary petition form rules by a stated deadline; it does not decide petition merits or impose a broader prospective filing ban.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam opinion joined by White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Souter; five of the eight participating Justices.

**Material application:** The present submissions were found frivolous; the applicants' extraordinary filing histories supported the finding of continuing abuse.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The ruling is discretionary. It adopts no numerical threshold, does not make earlier denials alone conclusive, and decides no underlying claim or constitutional entitlement to unrestricted free filing.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The seventeen pending fee-waiver motions are denied, with an opportunity to pay docketing fees and submit petitions in the required form by November 25, 1991. Ordinary screening remains available upon compliance.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** In re McDonald and In re Sindram are applied as authority for tailored protection of judicial process; neither is extended to a categorical prospective ban.

#### Vey v. Clinton

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-8796.

**Decided:** 1997-06-09.

##### A frivolous present petition may receive discretionary denial despite assumed financial eligibility

**Holding:** Rule 39.8 permits discretionary denial of financial leave when the particular petition is frivolous or malicious, even assuming financial eligibility. This petition supplies no arguable federal ground for disturbing the Third Circuit's screening dismissal or the refusal to reopen the dismissed action; financial leave is denied, with the paid filing opportunity stated in the order.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of the per curiam opinion, joined by all Justices except Stevens; eight direct joins.

**Material application:** The petition and attachments furnish no arguable federal basis to disturb the screening dismissal or refusal to reopen. Independent access allegations receive their own review. Repeated judges and adverse rulings alone do not establish partiality; self-representation does not guarantee full briefing before screening, and absence of counsel creates no categorical appointment entitlement in this civil collateral matter.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Concrete underlying injury allegations are neither declared incredible nor adjudicated. The denial rests on this paper, not filing totals, statutory labels, pleading insufficiency alone, a blanket immunity rule or certiorari entitlement. Financial means are assumed; malice and underlying liability are not found.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Deny financial leave, 8–1, with a paid Rule 38/33.1 opportunity through June 30, 1997. This order neither grants nor denies certiorari.

##### A separate future filing condition requires applicant-specific and category-specific justification

**Holding:** A prospective noncriminal-certiorari fee-and-form condition requires demonstrated applicant-specific abuse, a justified fit to the restricted filing category, and support for the inadequacy of considering particular papers. This record does not establish those predicates, and no new condition issues.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of the per curiam opinion, unanimous.

**Material application:** The mixed account of 26 unsuccessful submissions establishes neither relevant contents and repetition nor inadequacy of individual consideration in the noncriminal-certiorari channel. A present denial or retained paid route supplies no missing predicate. The earlier In re Vey order imposed no extraordinary-writ condition whose violation could be inferred.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No numerical threshold, universal warning requirement, exhaustion of every lesser measure or immunity from supported future control is adopted.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Decline the proposed new prospective noncriminal-certiorari condition. Financial leave, certiorari and original-writ entitlement remain distinct.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Martin v. District of Columbia Court of Appeals and Martin v. McDermott: applies distinct particular-paper and prospective-control inquiries; the arguable-paper grant and supported companion denial retain their separate scopes.
- Shieh v. Kakita and companions: applies separate means, current-content and applicant/category/inadequacy requirements; particular financial clarification remains permissible on a supported ground.
- Jones v. ABC-TV: applies independent examination of present access content; underlying defects do not automatically resolve it.
- In re Vey, April 14, 1997: distinguishes its arguable psychiatric-record and reopening contention; preserves its financial grant and refusal of an extraordinary-writ condition without disposing of that original proceeding.
- Liteky v. United States: applies objective appearance from the informed observer's standpoint; repeated judicial exposure and adverse decisions alone supply no concrete partiality circumstance here.
- Denton v. Hernandez: preserves the line between clearly baseless facts and ordinary disbelief; does not transplant the entire former statutory screening regime into Rule 39.8.
- Zatko v. California: preserves discretionary current-paper denial on demonstrated frivolousness, not a future ban.
- In re McDonald, In re Sindram and In re Demos: preserve targeted extraordinary-writ authority on supported records without transferring another applicant's abuse findings to Vey.

### Fee-waiver screening and appellate review

#### Denton v. Hernandez, 504 U.S. 25 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1846.
**Decided:** May 4, 1992.

##### When a complaint filed without prepayment of fees may be dismissed as factually frivolous

**Holding:** A court may dismiss a claim brought in forma pauperis (without paying filing fees first) as factually frivolous under 28 U.S.C. §1915(d) when its allegations are clearly baseless—irrational or wholly incredible—even without judicially noticeable facts contradicting them. The initial assessment must favor the plaintiff; mere improbability, extraordinary circumstances, or ordinary disbelief does not suffice, and screening cannot resolve genuine factual disputes.

**Proposition-level authority:** O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone, White, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Thomas. These seven Justices adopt the rule and the judgment-supporting explanation. Stevens’s dissent, joined by Blackmun, agrees with this factual-screening standard but disagrees with the additional appellate remand; their agreement is not needed to establish controlling authority.

**Current-force modification:** The clearly-baseless factual-screening rule remains the holding; the original permission to dismiss states former §1915(d). PLRA §804 replaces the dismissal text with §1915(e)(2), which requires dismissal at any time, notwithstanding payment of all or part of a filing fee, if the poverty allegation is untrue or the action or appeal is frivolous, malicious, fails to state a claim, or seeks monetary relief from an immune defendant. Admission of that command neither finds any claim frivolous nor supplies a new judicial construction of its categories; disputed application to pending proceedings remains unresolved.

**Material application:** The magistrate relied on the complaints’ combined pattern while acknowledging that each was not necessarily frivolous. Particular supporting affidavits must be considered without deciding truth from the pattern alone.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No particular district dismissal is affirmed, no allegation is found true or false, and no liability or damages are determined. The circuit’s categorical notice-and-amendment rule, an absolute right to amend and a universal preclusion rule for later fee applications remain undecided. Availability of an affidavit is not a condition of an arguable claim.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The appellate judgment is vacated and remanded, 7–2, for review of Nos. CIV S-83-0645, CIV S-83-1348 and CIV S-85-0084 under the proper standards. Review must consider the Esquer affidavit, proposed Pierce affidavit and amendment, pro se status and aggregate-pattern rationale. The two unappealed dismissals and due-process and mattress claims outside the grant are not reopened.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Neitzke v. Williams, 490 U.S. 319 (1989): applied and clarified; its clearly-baseless category reaches irrational or wholly incredible allegations without requiring a judicially noticeable contradiction, while preserving the distinction from Rule 12(b)(6).
- Haines v. Kerner, 404 U.S. 519 (1972): applied; liberal treatment of pleadings filed without counsel remains part of the screening inquiry and does not adjudicate their truth.

**Later-authority backlinks:** Prison Litigation Reform Act of 1995, Pub. L. 104-134, Title VIII, enacted April 26, 1996: §804(a), replacement mandatory dismissal provision §1915(e)(2). This is a noncase-law change, not an overruling or new judicial holding.

##### How appellate courts review a §1915(d) dismissal and account for its consequences

**Holding:** A dismissal under section 1915(d) is reviewed for abuse of discretion; an erroneous legal premise remains independently correctable as law. Review must address relevant circumstances, including pro se status, resolution of genuine factual disputes, legal errors, the adequacy of the stated reasons, prejudice, and whether more specific pleading could cure the defect; the dismissal is not a merits adjudication barring a paid complaint, although its possible effect on later in forma pauperis frivolousness determinations makes prejudice and curability material.

**Proposition-level authority:** O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone, White, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Thomas. Seven participating Justices adopt this review rule and its remand instructions. No separate proposition establishes a categorical preclusion rule for every later fee application or an absolute right to amend.

**Current-force modification:** The review and nonmerits-consequence holding governs the former §1915(d) dismissal adjudicated. PLRA §804 replaces that discretionary dismissal provision with the mandatory grounds described in the preceding question. The enactment itself does not settle the appellate standard, amendment opportunity or preclusive consequences under the new text. Its separate §1915(g) condition bars a prisoner from bringing a civil action or appealing a judgment in a civil action or proceeding under §1915 after the prisoner, on three or more prior occasions while incarcerated or detained in any facility, brought a federal action or appeal that was dismissed as frivolous, malicious or failing to state a claim, unless the prisoner is under imminent danger of serious physical injury; it does not establish that any Denton dismissal qualifies or bar a paid complaint automatically. Pending-case application remains unresolved.

**Material application:** Abuse-of-discretion review does not insulate legal error. Screening does not adjudicate the merits or bar a paid complaint; possible effects on later fee applications make prejudice and curability relevant.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No particular district dismissal is affirmed, no allegation is found true or false, and no liability or damages are determined. The circuit’s categorical notice-and-amendment rule, an absolute right to amend and a universal preclusion rule for later fee applications remain undecided. Availability of an affidavit is not a condition of an arguable claim.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The appellate judgment is vacated and remanded, 7–2, for review of Nos. CIV S-83-0645, CIV S-83-1348 and CIV S-85-0084 under the proper standards. Review must consider the Esquer affidavit, proposed Pierce affidavit and amendment, pro se status and aggregate-pattern rationale. The two unappealed dismissals and due-process and mattress claims outside the grant are not reopened.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Boag v. MacDougall, 454 U.S. 364 (1982): applied; statutory discretion does not preserve a dismissal resting on legal error, and reasons must permit intelligent appellate review.
- Haines v. Kerner, 404 U.S. 519 (1972): applied to review of pleadings filed without counsel and the significance of potentially curable deficiencies.
- Neitzke v. Williams, 490 U.S. 319 (1989): applied; section 1915(d)’s screening function remains distinct from disposition of the merits under ordinary pleading rules.

**Later-authority backlinks:** Prison Litigation Reform Act of 1995, Pub. L. 104-134, Title VIII, enacted April 26, 1996: §804(a), (d), replacement screening and the separate three-prior-dismissals condition. This is a noncase-law change, not an overruling or new judicial holding.

### Finality and earlier-period takings claims

#### Lucas v. South Carolina Coastal Council, 505 U.S. 1003 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-453.
**Decided:** June 29, 1992.

For the compensation rule, see Lucas v. South Carolina Coastal Council, June 29, 1992, No. 91-453, under Property and Economic Rights — Complete economic deprivation of land.

##### The original categorical prohibition presents a reviewable earlier-period claim

**Holding:** A later discretionary permit procedure does not bar review of compensation for an earlier categorical building prohibition where permission under that prohibition was unavailable and the state supreme court finally rejected the compensation claim on its merits. Any compensable taking must be remedied for its actual duration; the later procedure and unresolved factual issues remain relevant to future use, duration and valuation, and the permanent-value award is not automatically reinstated.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Opinion of the Court, Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela, White, O'Connor and Thomas, controls with five votes. Kennedy separately concurs in the judgment and agrees that the earlier interval is reviewable, but does not join the Court's writing. The holding rests on the express five-Justice opinion without combining separate rationales.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the no-compensation judgment and remand to the Supreme Court of South Carolina. The new permit avenue, remaining defenses, extent and duration of loss, and valuation remain for further proceedings; the permanent-value award is not automatically restored.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Williamson County Regional Planning Commission v. Hamilton Bank, 473 U.S. 172 (1985): preserves ordinary finality requirements; distinguishes an absolute prohibition for an already elapsed period.
- MacDonald, Sommer & Frates v. Yolo County, 477 U.S. 340 (1986): preserves the need to determine permitted development; an unavailable original permit need not be sought to test the earlier prohibition.
- First English Evangelical Lutheran Church v. County of Los Angeles, 482 U.S. 304 (1987): applies compensation for an actual temporary taking; does not decide whether this regulation effected one or set the amount.
- Yee v. City of Escondido, simulated April 1, 1992: applies the distinction between a presented claim and a distinct unpresented taking theory; no new physical-taking rule follows.

### Preservation and question-presented limits

#### Yee v. City of Escondido, 503 U.S. 519 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1947.
**Decided:** April 1, 1992.

For this case’s other questions, see *Yee v. City of Escondido*, April 1, 1992, No. 90-1947: whether restrictions on voluntarily created pad tenancies compel permanent physical occupation (Property and Economic Rights — Physical takings and voluntary tenancies).

##### Whether the unpreserved substantive-due-process claim is reviewable

**Holding:** The Court declines a substantive due-process claim neither properly raised nor decided in the state courts; presentation only in a denied request for discretionary review does not supply the missing state-court decision. The Court need not decide here whether this review limitation is jurisdictional or prudential.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II-A of O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas; six Justices adopt this procedural proposition. Stone does not join this rationale.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Due-process merits are not rejected. Whether the review limitation is jurisdictional or prudential is expressly undecided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the California judgment rejecting the physical-taking claim; award no compensation on that theory. Decline the due-process and regulatory-taking merits without deciding their validity; any properly available regulatory or as-applied claim requires an appropriate proceeding, not a new merits remand here.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Illinois v. Gates, 462 U.S. 213 — applied: an unpresented state-court claim is not decided on review; the jurisdictional/prudential classification remains open.
- People v. Triggs, 8 Cal.3d 884 — applied only to the effect of denied California discretionary review, which expresses no merits determination.

##### Whether a new taking argument and this facial theory satisfy preservation and ripeness

**Holding:** A party that properly presents a federal taking claim below may offer a different supporting argument on review, subject to the separate limitation imposed by the question presented. Under the then-governing standards, this facial allegation that the ordinance never substantially advances a legitimate state interest is ripe without a rent-increase request because it does not depend on the particular property’s economic loss or compensation.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II-B–C of O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas; six Justices adopt this procedural proposition. Stone does not join this rationale.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The holding uses the then-governing facial substantial-advancement theory and does not decide regulatory-taking merits or override the separate question-presented limit.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the California judgment rejecting the physical-taking claim; award no compensation on that theory. Decline the due-process and regulatory-taking merits without deciding their validity; any properly available regulatory or as-applied claim requires an appropriate proceeding, not a new merits remand here.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Bankers Life & Casualty Co. v. Crenshaw, 486 U.S. 71 — applied: new arguments may support a preserved federal claim.
- Illinois v. Gates, 462 U.S. 213 — applied: argument and claim preservation are distinct.
- Williamson County Regional Planning Commission v. Hamilton Bank, 473 U.S. 172 — distinguished: the asserted facial theory does not require a property-specific rent determination.
- Agins v. Tiburon, 447 U.S. 255 — applied only to the then-current facial-ripeness proposition; no merits determination follows.
- Keystone Bituminous Coal Assn. v. DeBenedictis, 480 U.S. 470 — applied to distinguish facial allegations from property-specific application.

##### Whether the physical-taking question fairly includes regulatory-taking merits

**Holding:** Under Rule 14.1(a), a question asking whether the ordinance causes a physical taking does not fairly include the related but independent question whether it causes a regulatory taking. No exceptional urgency or economy justifies departing from that prudential limit here, so the regulatory merits remain undecided.

**Proposition-level authority:** O’Connor, II-D, joined by Stone, White, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas: seven votes; this section adopts no regulatory merits or ripeness conclusion.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Preserving a general taking claim does not enlarge the grant. Physical and regulatory taking questions are related but not subsidiary to one another, and no exceptional urgency or economy warrants enlargement here.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the California judgment rejecting the physical-taking claim; award no compensation on that theory. Decline the due-process and regulatory-taking merits without deciding their validity; any properly available regulatory or as-applied claim requires an appropriate proceeding, not a new merits remand here.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Stone v. Powell, 428 U.S. 465 — applied: departure from the question-presented rule remains exceptional.
- Lytle v. Household Manufacturing, Inc., 494 U.S. 545 — applied: developed arguments and lower-court consideration support disciplined review.
- American National Bank & Trust Co. v. Haroco, Inc., 473 U.S. 606 — applied by analogy: related questions are not necessarily fairly included within one another.

#### Atherton v. Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-928.

**Decided:** January 14, 1997.

For the other questions in Atherton v. Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, No. 95-928, decided January 14, 1997, see Business and Commercial Law - Insured-bank officer duties and federal liability minimum (State law supplies the ordinary corporate duty; Section 1821(k) establishes a federal minimum and preserves other rights; Vacatur requires further proceedings under the correct sources).

##### Review of the source-of-law argument

**Holding:** The Court may consider the argument that no federal common-law duty exists because it concerns the same restored federal claims and is included within the presented dispute over §1821(k)'s displacement or preservation of those claims. Both sides have addressed that legal question on the existing record; consideration introduces no separate claim, additional factual finding or different requested relief.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's Part I, joined by all seven other Associates; eight Justices. Stone-Zsela agrees only to reach the merits.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The included legal issue does not enlarge the grant, displace an independently adverse judgment or create a universal antecedent-question exception. Withdrawal of state claims remains distinct.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Yee's argument/question distinction, United States National Bank's governing-law authority and Lebron's discretionary included-argument rule are applied within their limits; Izumi's separate-judgment limit is distinguished and preserved.

#### Walters v. Metropolitan Educational Enterprises, Inc. / Equal Employment Opportunity Commission v. Metropolitan Educational Enterprises, Inc.

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 95-259 and 95-779.

**Decided:** January 14, 1997.

For the other questions in Walters v. Metropolitan Educational Enterprises, Inc. / Equal Employment Opportunity Commission v. Metropolitan Educational Enterprises, Inc., Nos. 95-259 and 95-779, decided January 14, 1997, see Labor and Employment - Title VII employee counting (Count genuine employment relationships on each working day; The stipulated minimum satisfies the 1990 numerical requirement; The remand concerns retaliation, with the numerical objection resolved).

##### The unpresented promotion-year issue receives no relief

**Holding:** The Court declines to review the separate coverage-year discrepancy for Walters's September 1989 promotion claim because she raised no objection to it in her petition or merits brief and no supported exceptional reason warrants review. The existing promotion dismissal remains undisturbed on that prudential basis, without deciding its substantive correctness, an unknown 1988 count or intentional waiver.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Part III, joined by all seven other Associates; eight Justices. Stone-Zsela agrees only with leaving the dismissal undisturbed.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Nonreview is prudential, not a decision that 1990 is a proper promotion year, that the claim fails on its merits, that there was intentional waiver or that jurisdiction bars review. No 1988 count is inferred.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Yee and Zafiro support bounded nonreview and its undisturbed-judgment consequence; Williams, United States National Bank and Lebron retain their permissive, circumstance-specific scope and supply no mandatory promotion remand.

#### Turner Broadcasting System, Inc. v. Federal Communications Commission

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-992.
**Decided:** March 31, 1997.

For the full-power carriage merits, see *Turner*, March 31, 1997, No. 95-992 (First Amendment — Cable carriage and editorial discretion).

##### Whether the limited appeal decides separate low-power and market-modification classifications

**Holding:** Review confined to the full-power carriage scheme's advancement and fit under intermediate scrutiny does not decide the distinct content classifications of §534(h)(2)(B)'s low-power eligibility or §534(h)(1)(C)(ii)(III)'s market-modification factor. An earlier remand and present merits briefing do not enlarge the express grant, and the Court declines discretionary expansion.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's Opinion of the Court, Part I, unanimous.

**Material application:** Time Warner actually presents both objections; the Government's identified preservation objection concerns only low power. Neither separate classification is necessary to resolve the accepted full-power record question. The market factor concerns another eligible station's community news, sports, or events coverage, rather than a condition on the requesting station.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The 1995 Rules 18.3, 14.1(a), and 24.1(a) permit, but do not compel, otherwise available plain-error review. The Court finds neither categorical lack of standing nor abandonment, and decides neither provision's neutrality, validity, or severability. Benevolent motive and viewpoint neutrality do not themselves answer content classification.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Turner* (1994) classified the general full-power core while reserving these distinct provisions; this limited appeal does not silently resolve them.

### Preservation and questions passed upon below

#### United States v. Williams, 504 U.S. 36 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1972.
**Decided:** May 4, 1992.

For the other questions in *United States v. Williams*, May 4, 1992, No. 90-1972, see Federal Courts — Preservation and questions passed upon below (reviewability) and Criminal Procedure — Grand-jury presentation and supervisory dismissal (disclosure duty and dismissal authority).

##### Whether the Court may review a federal question passed upon below despite failure to renew an objection to circuit precedent

**Holding:** The Court may review an important federal question expressly decided by the court of appeals even though a party did not renew its challenge to binding circuit precedent in this case, where adherence to that precedent did not concede its correctness. This is a permissible exercise of review, not an obligation to decide every unpressed question or a rule excusing an affirmative waiver.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone’s reviewability opinion, joined by White, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter: five Justices adopt the rule and explanation. The four Justices who prefer dismissal of the writ do not join this rule.

**Material application:** The Tenth Circuit expressly applied Page. Adhering to binding precedent did not concede its correctness; the Government had opposed the rule when it was established.

**Limits and questions not reached:** This discretionary review does not displace trial-objection requirements, excuse affirmative waiver or decide review standards for state judgments.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Court reaches the question, 5–4: Stone, White, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter; Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor and Thomas prefer dismissal of the writ.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Stevens v. Department of Treasury, 500 U.S. 1 — applied: actual decision below can satisfy the pressed-or-passed-upon practice.
- United States v. Page, 808 F.2d 723 — its binding force within the Tenth Circuit explains the litigation posture; adherence does not establish agreement with its correctness.

#### United States v. Wells

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-1228.
**Decided:** February 26, 1997.

For the separate questions in United States v. Wells, No. 95-1228 decided February 26, 1997, see Criminal Procedure — Bank false statements and statutory elements (Section 1014 does not add an objective materiality element).

##### The Court may review the statutory question actually decided below

**Holding:** The Government may argue that §1014 lacks a materiality element where that legal question was presented in appellate briefing, decided by the court of appeals and included in the question granted, despite its earlier request for instructions under then-prevailing circuit law. This bounded choice to review statutory meaning does not excuse every waiver or decide the independent effect of the indictment, instructions or case-specific prejudice.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of the Opinion of the Court (Thomas), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Rule 30, invited error and independent consequences of indictment language or the actual instructions may still matter to properly available claims. The Court's review choice does not certify their preservation or resolve prejudice.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Lebron permits an included legal argument on an adequate record; the Government's supplemental appellate briefing, decision below and granted question satisfy the bounded rationale here without creating a universal waiver exception.

### Enforcement, modification and summary judgment in original actions

#### Nebraska v. Wyoming, 507 U.S. 584 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 108, Original.

**Decided:** April 20, 1993.

##### Enforcement and modification require different showings

**Holding:** Enforcement of an established decree right requires no new showing of injury; the right may be identified from the decree, original opinion, report, record and relevant course of conduct. A request for new allocation or modification requires clear and convincing evidence of real and substantial injury; Nebraska’s express invocation of Paragraph XIII permits that request despite some enforcement language in its pleadings.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor’s Opinion of the Court, I; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Wyoming v. Colorado, 309 U.S. 572 — applied: enforcement does not require a fresh injury showing.
- Wyoming v. Colorado, 286 U.S. 494 — applied: original materials may explain decree rights.
- Idaho ex rel. Evans, 462 U.S. 1017 — applied: clear and convincing real and substantial injury for new relief.
- Arizona v. California, 460 U.S. 605 — applied: stability weighs against reopening without substantial justification.

##### Summary judgment uses the applicable substantive burden

**Holding:** Under Rule 17.2, Rule 56 and its precedents guide summary disposition in this original action: judgment requires no genuine dispute of material fact and entitlement as a matter of law. A nonmovant bearing the eventual burden must make a sufficient showing on essential elements, evaluated in light of the applicable enforcement or clear-and-convincing modification standard.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor’s Opinion of the Court, I; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Celotex, 477 U.S. 317 — applied as a guide to failure of proof on an essential element.
- Anderson, 477 U.S. 242 — applied as a guide: materiality and sufficiency reflect the substantive burden.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Enforcement and modification burdens remain distinct; Rule 56 is a guide under Rule 17.2. The Grayrocks settlement binds its parties but incorporation is undecided; no present interference with its minimum releases is shown. Paragraph XIII already permits warranted new protection. The exact scope of Paragraph X, project injury, excessive calls and remaining downstream factual questions are not resolved.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Grant the Inland Lakes and limited no-canal-cap partial judgments; deny the remaining summary-judgment motions and overrule all exceptions. No new final river decree or injunction issues. Necessary temporary delivery storage preserves the recognized quantity without altering other Guernsey or Glendo rights; the acquiescence alternative has eight votes and no Stone join.

For Nebraska v. Wyoming, April 20, 1993, No. 108, Original, concerning interstate water decrees and equitable allocation, see **Property and Economic Rights — Interstate water decrees and equitable allocation**.

#### Nebraska v. Wyoming

**Docket or dockets:** No. 108, Original.
**Decided:** May 30, 1995.

For the other questions in Nebraska v. Wyoming, decided May 30, 1995, No. 108, Original. see Property and Economic Rights — Interstate water decrees and equitable allocation (Settled allocation and discrete defenses).

##### Groundwater and equitable connection

**Holding:** Specific upstream groundwater development and alleged depletion threatening the decree allocation may be pleaded. Nebraska's own pumping creates no categorical unclean-hands bar without relevant conduct and connection to the asserted harm; supported defenses and applicable eventual proof requirements remain.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join this proposition.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Enforcement of an established right requires no fresh injury showing; modification requires clear and convincing evidence of real and substantial injury. Leave proves neither causation nor injury and confers no general supervision over every well. Kansas v. Colorado supplies no imported Compact elements or factual conclusions.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Nebraska v. Wyoming, April 20, 1993: applies distinct enforcement and modification burdens; modification requires clear and convincing real and substantial injury.
- Kansas v. Colorado, May 15, 1995: applies proof-sensitive pumping and defense analysis without importing Compact elements or factual conclusions.

##### Downstream and habitat evidence

**Holding:** Downstream nonirrigation and habitat effects may be considered insofar as relevant to injury and lawful modification in admitted decree claims. Relevance creates no independent instream entitlement, habitat-flow quantity, free-standing environmental right or automatic priority; Count IV remains denied without prejudice.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join this proposition.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Count IV's broader seasonal theory remains denied without prejudice. Habitat and downstream evidence must be relevant to the admitted decree claims and satisfy any applicable modification burden; evidence of injury does not itself create a new allocation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Nebraska v. Wyoming, 325 U.S. 589: supplies the allocation setting without independent habitat rights.
- Nebraska v. Wyoming, April 20, 1993: applies distinct enforcement and modification burdens; modification requires clear and convincing real and substantial injury.

##### Horse Creek and supporting conditions

**Holding:** Horse Creek's location below Tri-State Dam does not alone bar a specific changed-condition allegation threatening the decree's balance. A material decree connection and eventual modification proof remain required; no tributary-wide apportionment or finding of depletion follows.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join this proposition.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The downstream location does not itself prove or defeat the allegation. A material connection to conditions supporting the decree and clear and convincing real and substantial injury for modification remain necessary.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Nebraska v. Wyoming, 325 U.S. 589: applies the allocation setting and preserves geographic limits.
- Nebraska v. Wyoming, April 20, 1993: applies distinct enforcement and modification burdens; modification requires clear and convincing real and substantial injury.

##### Federal administration as decree predicate

**Holding:** The Fourth Cross-Claim may proceed only for alleged changed federal storage administration materially affecting the interstate decree's operation or supporting conditions. Both United States and Nebraska exceptions are overruled within that scope; independent contractual compliance remains outside the original proceeding and in its lawful separate channel.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Opinion of the Court, federal-administration portion; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer directly join this proposition.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Storage water is not directly apportioned. Admission establishes no general immunity waiver, contractual breach, damages claim or absent party's contract rights, and displaces no separate contract litigation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Nebraska v. Wyoming, 325 U.S. 589, and 345 U.S. 981: preserve allocation and retained jurisdiction without allocating storage water.
- Texas v. New Mexico, 462 U.S. 554: applies interstate enforcement within operative scope, not general contract jurisdiction.
- Nebraska v. Wyoming, April 20, 1993: applies distinct enforcement and modification burdens; modification requires clear and convincing real and substantial injury.

##### Exact leave and next stage

**Holding:** Permit Nebraska Counts I-III, Wyoming Second-Fourth Counterclaims and Wyoming Second, Third and Fifth Cross-Claims unanimously within the preceding limits; permit the Fourth Cross-Claim within its decree-effects limit by eight votes, with Thomas opposed. Deny Nebraska Count IV without prejudice and reject only impermissible ceiling portions of First Counterclaim and First Cross-Claim. Return Glendo, Laramie and carriage-loss theories to the Master for applicable proof; leave establishes no injury, breach, water award, priority, damages or injunction.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer join the other leave dispositions and next-stage directions. Only Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer join admission of the Fourth Cross-Claim; Thomas dissents from that admission.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Amendment leave is not proof or summary judgment. Rule 56 under Rule 17.2 remains only a guide to summary disposition; the distinct enforcement and modification burdens and supported defenses remain.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Nebraska v. Wyoming, April 20, 1993: applies distinct enforcement and modification burdens; modification requires clear and convincing real and substantial injury. Rule 56 remains a limited guide; leave is not proof.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Overrule Wyoming's four exceptions unanimously and the United States' and Nebraska's exceptions to the Fourth Cross-Claim 8–1 (Thomas would sustain those two). Allow Nebraska Counts I–III and Wyoming Second–Fourth Counterclaims and Second–Fifth Cross-Claims only within the admitted scope; deny Nebraska Count IV without prejudice. Reject the First Counterclaim and First Cross-Claim only insofar as they seek the mass ceiling, preserving discrete below-Tri-State calls, priority bypasses and relevant waste defenses. Return the Glendo enforcement/modification, Laramie changed-condition and carriage-loss theories for proof before the Master. No injury, breach, new allocation, release quantity, revised formula, seasonal award, damages, priority or operating injunction is adjudicated.

### Exclusive original jurisdiction and private title litigation

#### Mississippi v. Louisiana, 506 U.S. 73 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-1158.

**Decided:** December 14, 1992.

##### An interstate complaint belongs to the exclusive original forum

**Holding:** Section 1251(a) denies a federal district court jurisdiction to grant relief on an actual controversy between two states, even when the complaint is joined to private litigation and this Court previously declined an original action. The appellate judgment is reversed insofar as it adjudicates Louisiana’s complaint against Mississippi, with instructions to direct dismissal of that complaint for want of jurisdiction.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela’s opinion, joined by the other eight Justices; unanimous controlling holding.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- California v. Arizona, 440 U.S. 59 — applied; this Court is the exclusive federal forum for a state’s claim against another state.
- Illinois v. City of Milwaukee, 406 U.S. 91 — distinguished as to different defendants and applied to preserve original-forum discretion.
- Ohio v. Wyandotte Chemicals Corp., 401 U.S. 493 — preserved; discretion does not grant another court exclusive interstate jurisdiction.
- Texas v. New Mexico, 462 U.S. 554 — preserved; case-specific discretion does not negate §1251(a).
- Wyoming v. Oklahoma, January 22, 1992 — distinguished; its original-forum exercise and separate objections supply no district-court authority here.

##### Private title questions remain distinct from binding interstate relief

**Holding:** Section 1251(a) does not bar an otherwise lawful private-title action merely because deciding title requires locating a state boundary; such adjudication does not bind the states on their boundary. Louisiana’s intervention that seeks no relief against Mississippi is unaffected, and the remaining title claims return for a determination whether the existing record permits fair resolution or additional proceedings are necessary.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela’s opinion, joined by the other eight Justices; unanimous controlling holding and remand limitation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Durfee v. Duke, 375 U.S. 106 — applied; adjudication between private parties does not bind the states on their boundary.
- California v. Arizona, 440 U.S. 59 — applied to the actual interstate controversy; it does not withdraw otherwise lawful private-title jurisdiction.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court decides no interstate boundary, private title, accretion, avulsion or sovereign acquiescence issue. Party agreement or convenience cannot create district-court jurisdiction over the interstate complaint.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the interstate adjudication and direct dismissal of Louisiana’s complaint against Mississippi for want of jurisdiction. Remand the remaining title claims to determine whether the record permits fair resolution or further district proceedings are needed; Louisiana’s nonadverse intervention is unaffected.

### Federal merits failure and pendent jurisdiction

#### Bray v. Alexandria Women’s Health Clinic, 506 U.S. 263 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-985.

**Decided:** January 13, 1993.

##### Federal merits failure does not retroactively destroy jurisdiction

**Holding:** These §1985(3) claims were not wholly insubstantial and frivolous, so their failure on the merits does not remove original federal jurisdiction. Power over the related state claims remains subject to the governing pendent-jurisdiction principles rather than automatic denial because of today’s federal merits ruling.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia Part IV, joined by Stone-Zsela, White, Kennedy and Thomas: five direct supporters. This does not establish entitlement to any particular injunction.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Bell v. Hood, 327 U.S. 678 — applied; substantial federal jurisdiction differs from failure on the merits.
- United Mine Workers v. Gibbs, 383 U.S. 715 — preserved and applied to pendent jurisdiction in this pre-§1367 action.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Women’s status as a qualifying class, invidious sex-based animus, the full nonracial class boundary, a categorical rule for equal intrastate obstruction and statutory authority for an injunction on a successful federal claim remain unresolved. Scalia Part III has four votes and supplies no alternative holding. The hindrance theory receives no merits ruling; a later properly pleaded and supported claim is not barred on its merits by this disposition.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse deprivation-clause liability, vacate the $27,687.55 federal fee-and-cost award and remand for the injunction’s state trespass/public-nuisance grounds, pendent jurisdiction and lawful scope. Federal merits failure does not automatically dissolve the injunction or dismiss the entire action for lack of jurisdiction. The five-Justice mandate directs no hindrance adjudication.

For Bray v. Alexandria Women’s Health Clinic, January 13, 1993, No. 90-985, concerning private conspiracies and clinic obstruction, see **Civil Rights — Private conspiracies and clinic obstruction**.

### Fee access and abusive filings

#### Demos v. Storrie, 507 U.S. 290 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-6846.

**Decided:** March 8, 1993.

##### The official-interference allegation is not defeated by filing history

**Holding:** Rule 39.8 permits denial of leave to file without prepayment only upon a finding that the particular petition is frivolous or malicious; a documented pattern of prior unsuccessful filings does not substitute for that finding. Demos's allegation that official interference prevented a timely appeal of the underlying prefiling order is a particular, non-conclusory claim that clears the nonfrivolousness threshold on this record, so the Court grants leave without deciding that the allegation warrants certiorari or would succeed on the merits.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam opinion, joined by all nine Justices; all nine participating Justices adopt this filing-specific application and its limits.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Zatko v. California, 502 U.S. 16 (November 4, 1991): applied as the existing discretionary, particular-submission rule; its extreme-abuse finding does not establish the frivolousness of a different current petition.
- Martin v. District of Columbia Court of Appeals, No. 92-5584 (November 2, 1992): applied; its filing-specific frivolousness standard controls this later fee motion.
- In re Demos, 500 U.S. 16 (1991): distinguished; its narrower, earlier extraordinary-writ restriction addressed different filings and does not itself resolve this civil-certiorari fee question.
- Denton v. Hernandez, 504 U.S. 25 (May 4, 1992): its distinction between an arguable claim and a clearly baseless allegation informs this limited assessment without importing its statutory screening rules wholesale into Rule 39.

##### No prospective filing restriction on this record

**Holding:** The Court declines to impose a prospective fee-and-form condition on Demos's unidentified future civil-certiorari petitions because the established record does not show that individualized treatment of particular filings is inadequate. This case-specific refusal decides neither that a prior warning is invariably required nor that prospective restrictions are categorically unlawful.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam opinion, joined by all nine Justices; all nine participate.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- In re McDonald, 489 U.S. 180 (1989): preserved as authority for targeted protection against extraordinary-writ abuse; no extension to all future civil-certiorari petitions is made here.
- In re Sindram, 498 U.S. 177 (1991): its extraordinary-writ setting is distinguished; its existing force is unchanged.
- Martin v. District of Columbia Court of Appeals (November 2, 1992): applied; the same unanimous decline to impose a prospective restriction controls here.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No finding establishes official interference, excuse of the unappealed default or merit in the underlying §1983 action. Existing extraordinary-writ restrictions are distinct; no universal warning requirement or numerical filing threshold is adopted.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Grant the fee motion and decline prospective restrictions. Denial of certiorari in the ordinary course supplies no merits precedent.

#### Martin v. District of Columbia Court of Appeals and Martin v. McDermott, 506 U.S. 1 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-5584; No. 92-5618.

**Decided:** November 2, 1992.

##### No. 92-5584: an arguable current petition is not defeated by filing history

**Holding:** Rule 39.8 permits denial of leave to file without prepayment only upon a finding that the particular petition is frivolous or malicious; earlier unsuccessful filings cannot substitute for that finding. No. 92-5584's distinct general-rule and prospective statutory arguments clear the nonfrivolousness threshold on this record, so the Court grants leave without deciding that the arguments warrant certiorari or would succeed on the merits.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of the per curiam opinion, joined by all nine Justices; all nine participating Justices adopt this filing-specific application and its limits.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Zatko v. California, 502 U.S. 16 (November 4, 1991): applied as the existing discretionary, particular-submission rule; its extreme-abuse finding does not establish the frivolousness of a different current petition.
- District of Columbia Court of Appeals v. Feldman, 460 U.S. 462 (1983): its general-rule/individual-judgment distinction supplies an arguable legal question here; the Court does not decide which side of that distinction the complete complaint occupies.
- Denton v. Hernandez, 504 U.S. 25 (May 4, 1992): its distinction between arguability and clearly baseless factual allegations informs this limited assessment; its statutory review and remedial rules remain unchanged and are not imported wholesale into Rule 39.

##### No. 92-5618: particular frivolousness and the available paid route

**Holding:** Where the current recusal petition identifies no concrete constitutionally disqualifying interest and develops no federal challenge to the independent state jurisdictional ground that defeats the action, those defects together establish frivolousness for this Rule 39.8 motion. In light of Martin's established extreme repetitive filing abuse, the Court exercises its discretion to deny this motion only, allowing payment of the Rule 38 fee and submission in Rule 33 form through November 23, 1992; the petition itself is not denied by this order.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of the per curiam opinion, joined by White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; six of nine control the motion, its stated ground and the compliance opportunity. Stone-Zsela, Blackmun and Stevens dissent from this part.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Zatko v. California, 502 U.S. 16 (November 4, 1991): applied to this distinct submission; fee denial remains discretionary and separate from disposition of certiorari.
- Aetna Life Insurance Co. v. Lavoie, 475 U.S. 813 (1986): distinguished because this petition establishes no comparable concrete personal financial stake; other constitutionally disqualifying interests are not categorically excluded.

##### No prospective filing restriction on this record

**Holding:** The Court declines to impose a prospective fee-and-form condition on Martin's unidentified future civil-certiorari petitions because the established record does not show that the available treatment of particular filings is inadequate. This case-specific refusal decides neither that a prior warning is invariably required nor that prospective restrictions are categorically unlawful, and imposes no condition on future civil or criminal filings.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III of the per curiam opinion, joined by all nine Justices; all nine adopt the sufficiency of this narrower, record-specific ground without resolving the Court's power on a different record.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- In re McDonald, 489 U.S. 180 (1989): preserved as authority for targeted protection against extraordinary-writ abuse; no extension to all future civil-certiorari petitions is made here.
- In re Sindram, 498 U.S. 177 (1991): its extraordinary-writ setting and preservation of other filing categories are distinguished; its existing force is unchanged.
- Zatko v. California, 502 U.S. 16 (November 4, 1991): its limited remedy is preserved; no warning, compliance default or further adjudication is inferred from that order.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No merits ruling on bar admission, ADA or Civil Rights Act coverage or retroactivity, immunity, the full complaint's jurisdictional status, or recusal follows. The two present defects in the companion operate together; neither professional affiliation, filing volume nor a state characterization alone establishes frivolousness. No universal warning prerequisite or categorical prohibition of prospective restrictions is adopted.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The lead fee motion is granted. The companion fee motion is denied with leave to pay the Rule 38 fee and submit a Rule 33 petition by November 23, 1992; that opportunity neither denies certiorari nor commands automatic denial upon passage of the date. No prospective restriction issues. The lead certiorari denial supplies no merits holding.

### Forfeiture jurisdiction and effective monetary relief

#### Republic National Bank of Miami v. United States, 506 U.S. 80 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-767.

**Decided:** December 14, 1992.

##### Removal does not itself terminate perfected forfeiture jurisdiction

**Holding:** Once valid attachment has established in rem jurisdiction, removal of the property or substituted proceeds does not by itself defeat appellate jurisdiction. The bank’s failure to obtain a stay or bond therefore does not foreclose this appeal where a favorable judgment could still provide lawful effectual relief; an entirely useless judgment presents a distinct limit.

**Proposition-level authority:** Parts I, II and IV of Blackmun’s opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, White, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter; eight Justices adopt this rule. Thomas supports the judgment on a different statutory ground.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- The Rio Grande, 23 Wall. 458 — applied; removal alone does not terminate jurisdiction properly acquired.
- The Little Charles, 26 F. Cas. 979 — applied; the ability to effectuate relief is distinct from uninterrupted custody.
- The Brig Ann, 9 Cranch 289 — distinguished; abandonment before institution differs from removal after valid attachment.
- Taylor v. Carryl, 20 How. 583 — distinguished; initial jurisdiction does not establish perpetual custody as a condition.
- Church of Scientology of California v. United States, November 16, 1992 — applied by analogy on effectual relief; its IRS-copy rule supplies no payment authority.

##### The return statute and judgment appropriation permit effective relief upon success

**Holding:** If the bank prevails in the underlying forfeiture and obtains the requisite judgment, 28 U.S.C. §2465 supplies a substantive right to return of the substituted proceeds; 31 U.S.C. §1304 and 28 U.S.C. §2414 supply the judgment-payment route when payment is not otherwise provided, the Comptroller General certifies payment, and the obligation satisfies the statutory final-judgment and payable-judgment requirements. That conditional lawful route defeats the Government’s claim that this appeal can produce no effective relief, without ordering payment now or recognizing judicial spending power apart from an applicable appropriation.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela’s statutory-payment opinion, joined by White, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas, supplies six express joins and independently controls. Stevens expressly accepts the same sufficient statutory proposition in his own writing, making seven supporters without an invented formal join.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Office of Personnel Management v. Richmond, 496 U.S. 414 — applied; a substantive statutory entitlement and applicable appropriation, not equity alone, are required for this payment route.
- Knote v. United States, 95 U.S. 149 — preserved; the statutory resolution leaves the broader constitutional question about contested forfeiture proceeds undecided.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The bank must establish its entitlement; no payment source or appropriation-free constitutional return theory is decided. The Court leaves §1355(c)'s application to pre-enactment transfers or appeals unresolved. Pub. L. 102-550 §1521 independently added §1355(b)–(d) effective October 28, 1992: subsection (c) preserves jurisdiction upon removal by the prevailing party in a case in which a final civil-forfeiture order is appealed and, upon the appellant's motion, requires an order necessary to protect full value, which may include a stay or bond. This decision does not determine that amendment's temporal application.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the Eleventh Circuit jurisdictional dismissal and remand the forfeiture appeal; no immediate payment, ownership or lien decree.

### Organizational eligibility for fee relief

#### Rowland v. California Men’s Colony, Unit II Men’s Advisory Council, 506 U.S. 194 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-1188.

**Decided:** January 12, 1993.

##### Associations may apply

**Holding:** An association is not categorically excluded from then-effective §1915: the Dictionary Act’s inclusion of associations within person applies because §1915’s affidavit, financial-inability and counsel provisions can operate through ordinary agency and representation rules. The context exception remains operative, but these features do not establish an incompatible statutory setting or authorize a natural-person-only restriction.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas’s Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens and Kennedy: five votes for this construction and its operative limits. Kennedy’s separate account of permissible workability analysis adds no condition to the Court’s holding.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- United States v. A & P Trucking Co., 358 U.S. 121 (1958): applied for giving statutory entity language effective operation through ordinary legal mechanisms; criminal-liability rules are not imported.
