- Wilson v. Omaha Indian Tribe, 442 U.S. 653 (1979): applied for the Dictionary Act’s inclusive default subject to actual context; no requirement of an express contrary definition is created.
- Neitzke v. Williams, 490 U.S. 319 (1989): applied to preserve statutory construction against a policy substitution based on administrative convenience.
- Mallard v. United States District Court, 490 U.S. 296 (1989): preserved; §1915(d) permits a request for counsel and does not compel service.
- Osborn v. Bank of the United States, 22 U.S. (9 Wheat.) 738 (1824): preserved; an entity’s lawful counsel requirement is separate from financial eligibility.

##### Eligibility does not establish the showing

**Holding:** An eligible association must submit a properly authorized affidavit establishing its actual inability to pay costs or give security and its asserted entitlement to redress; the court may examine legally available resources and sham concealment without automatically attributing every member’s assets to it. Inability to hold an entity bank account or a balance-sheet deficit alone does not compel fee relief, so the Council’s application returns for the actual financial and representation determinations; §1915 neither licenses lay entity advocacy nor guarantees appointed counsel.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas’s Opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens and Kennedy: five votes for this construction and its operative limits. Kennedy’s separate account of permissible workability analysis adds no condition to the Court’s holding. The dissenters would require denial because of entity status and do not join the Court’s application process.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Adkins v. E. I. DuPont de Nemours & Co., 335 U.S. 331 (1948): applied to actual financial inability; its individual-applicant facts do not categorically exclude associations or prescribe a corporate insolvency test.
- Osborn and Mallard: preserved as distinct counsel and representation limits rather than grounds for automatic financial disqualification.
- Neitzke and Denton v. Hernandez (May 4, 1992): preserved for their respective legal and factual screening rules, with Denton’s abuse-of-discretion review and independent correction of legal error unchanged.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Relief remains discretionary after the statutory showing. The Council’s restriction on holding funds is relevant but does not preclude examination of resources lawfully available through direct payment. No automatic aggregation of members’ assets, universal organizational insolvency formula, lay entity advocacy, compelled counsel, subsidized-litigation constitutional right or underlying tobacco-policy merits ruling is established.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm rejection of categorical association ineligibility, vacate the automatic fee grant and remand for an authorized affidavit, actual resources, lawful representation and statutory screening.

### Petitioner-specific appellate preservation

#### Zafiro v. United States, 506 U.S. 534 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-6824.

**Decided:** January 25, 1993.

##### Petitioner-specific omission on appeal

**Holding:** When Gloria Zafiro did not pursue her severance denial on appeal and the court of appeals did not decide her personal claim, the Court declines to consider that claim without a supported exceptional reason; her codefendants’ preservation supplies none. Her judgment remains undisturbed on that prudential basis, with no merits rejection, jurisdictional bar or finding of intentional constitutional waiver.

**Proposition-level authority:** O’Connor’s opinion for Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas; eight participating Justices adopt the explanation. Stevens agrees in the disposition but does not join the Court’s opinion.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Youakim v. Miller, 425 U.S. 231 — applied: nonreview is ordinary practice, not an inflexible jurisdictional rule.
- United States v. Williams, May 4, 1992 — distinguished at its stated scope: a passed-upon question may be reviewed; Gloria’s personal claim was not passed upon.
- Yee v. City of Escondido, April 1, 1992 — applied: an unrelated sufficiency question does not enter the granted severance review.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Serious risk may arise from another defendant’s evidence, markedly different culpability in complex proof or unavailable exculpatory evidence; the examples are not exhaustive. A better chance of acquittal alone is insufficient. Rule 8 joinder and independent confrontation rights remain distinct; review of effective Rule 14 relief is for abuse of discretion. Gloria’s sufficiency and collateral questions are not reached.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm on petitioner-specific grounds: Gloria’s claim is not reviewed; Garcia, Soto and Martinez receive no new trial because the individualized instructions adequately address the risks shown.

For Zafiro v. United States, January 25, 1993, No. 91-6824, concerning joint trials, severance and claim preservation, see **Criminal Procedure — Joint trials, severance and claim preservation**.

### Pleading municipal civil-rights claims

#### Leatherman v. Tarrant County Narcotics Intelligence and Coordination Unit, 507 U.S. 163 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-1657.

**Decided:** March 3, 1993.

##### The Federal Rules supply the pleading standard for municipal § 1983 claims

**Holding:** A federal court may not impose a judge-created heightened factual-particularity requirement on a § 1983 complaint against a municipality beyond the then-operative Federal Rules. Rule 8 governs the complaint, Rule 9(b)'s fraud-or-mistake categories do not create a municipal-liability category, and Monell and Canton retain their separate substantive requirements.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone's Opinion of the Court, joined in full by the other eight Justices; unanimous controlling authority.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Conley v. Gibson, 355 U.S. 41 (1957): applied for fair-notice pleading; no subsequent plausibility rule is imported.
- Monell v. Department of Social Services, 436 U.S. 658 (1978): preserved; municipal policy/custom and causation remain necessary, while freedom from employer-based liability supplies no immunity from suit.
- Owen v. City of Independence, 445 U.S. 622 (1980): applied to reject municipal qualified immunity and the pleading argument built on it.
- City of Canton v. Harris, 489 U.S. 378 (1989): preserved as substantive failure-to-train attribution law, not authority for greater pleading particularity.
- Elliott v. Perez, 751 F.2d 1472 (5th Cir.1985), and Palmer v. City of San Antonio, 810 F.2d 514 (5th Cir.1987): rejected insofar as their rule demands heightened particularity for municipal § 1983 complaints; individual-immunity pleading remains unresolved.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Ordinary Rule 8 and Rule 11 duties, discovery control and Rule 56 remain. Monell/Canton attribution and the underlying constitutional wrong are not relaxed. Individual-officer immunity pleading, collateral estoppel and unreviewed alternative summary-judgment grounds remain undecided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the Fifth Circuit and remand under the ordinary pleading rules; no municipal liability finding or new trial is ordered.

### Federal restitution and derivative claims

#### United States v. California, 507 U.S. 746 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-2003.
**Decided:** April 26, 1993.

##### Reimbursement does not create the asserted federal refund right

**Holding:** Federal reimbursement or advance funding of an independent contractor’s state taxes does not alone create a federal common-law action for money had and received to recover alleged state-law assessment errors. The contractor’s legal tax liability does not become a direct tax on the United States merely because the Treasury bears its economic cost.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- United States v. New Mexico, 455 U.S. 720: applies legal incidence/economic burden distinction.
- Brady v. Roosevelt S.S. Co., 317 U.S. 575: applies limits on altering third-party rights by indemnity.
- Bayne v. United States, 93 U.S. 642, and Gaines v. Miller, 111 U.S. 395: distinguished; unlawful diversion and recipient responsibility are absent.

##### Later subrogation does not revive the expired claim

**Holding:** Although indemnification gave the Government a right to seek subrogation, applicable California law required active assertion before substitution occurred; by the May 1988 federal suit, WBEC’s remaining refund claim had lapsed. The United States takes that derivative claim subject to its pre-existing infirmity; the Court does not decide generally whether state or federal limitations govern state-law claims brought by the United States.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Direct federal taxation, congressionally created remedies and the correctness of California’s tax classification are not decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm summary judgment for California on both asserted theories; the completed $3 million partial refund remains unaffected. No further refund or interest is awarded.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- United States v. Munsey Trust Co., 332 U.S. 234: applies derivative nature of subrogation.
- Offer v. Superior Court, 194 Cal.114: applies the state-law active-assertion requirement.
- Guaranty Trust Co. v. United States, 304 U.S. 126: applies pre-acquisition infirmity rule.
- United States v. Summerlin, 310 U.S. 414: distinguished and preserved for live federal claims against subsequent state limits.

### Independent patent counterclaims and appellate vacatur

#### Cardinal Chemical Co. v. Morton International, Inc., 508 U.S. 83 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-114.
**Decided:** May 17, 1993.

##### An intermediate noninfringement ruling does not terminate counterclaim jurisdiction

**Holding:** The Federal Circuit’s affirmance of noninfringement does not deprive it of jurisdiction over a live, independent invalidity counterclaim merely because its own ruling would resolve the infringement claim. That intermediate ruling remains subject to this Court’s review and does not justify treating validity review as automatically advisory.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Altvater v. Freeman, 319 U.S. 359: applies independent-counterclaim distinction.
- Electrical Fittings Corp. v. Thomas & Betts Co., 307 U.S. 241: distinguished; unnecessary affirmative-defense adjudication is different.

##### An established invalidity controversy does not disappear without a change

**Independent alternative holding:** Once an independent invalidity counterclaim presents an actual controversy, the party asserting later mootness must demonstrate a change that eliminates it. Affirmance of noninfringement alone does not establish that change or authorize automatic vacatur of the invalidity judgment.

**Proposition-level authority for alternative holding:** Stevens’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Altvater v. Freeman, 319 U.S. 359: applies independent-counterclaim distinction.
- Aetna Life Insurance Co. v. Haworth, 300 U.S. 227, and United States v. W.T. Grant Co., 345 U.S. 629: distinguishes initial controversy burden from later mootness burden.
- United States v. Munsingwear, Inc., 340 U.S. 36: actual intervening mootness rule preserved; automatic application rejected.
- INS v. Chadha, 462 U.S. 919: agreement on one legal issue does not eliminate underlying adversity.

##### The automatic practice cannot be saved as discretion on this record

**Holding:** The Federal Circuit may not routinely vacate a live invalidity judgment merely because it affirms noninfringement, nor save that same automatic practice by calling it discretionary. On this record the validity appeal should be considered; unusual case-specific grounds for declining review or actual mootness remain available, but no universal duty to decide validity in every patent appeal is imposed.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct majority agreement at this level of generality; no Marks inference.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Actual later mootness and lawful unusual-case discretion remain available; no universal command to decide validity, technical validity ruling or fee ruling follows. The first two jurisdictional grounds independently defeat automatic mootness; the seven-Justice discretionary holding is distinct.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate only the vacatur of the invalidity judgment and remand for consideration of the live validity appeal on this record; the noninfringement affirmance remains intact.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Blonder-Tongue Laboratories, Inc. v. University of Illinois Foundation, 402 U.S. 313: applies distinct public and preclusion significance of invalidity.
- Altvater, 319 U.S. 359: independent judgment cannot be discarded solely because infringement fails.

### Finality of a completed conviction ruling

#### Sullivan v. Louisiana, 508 U.S. 275 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-5129.
**Decided:** June 1, 1993.

For the other question-level holdings in Sullivan v. Louisiana, June 1, 1993, No. 92-5129, see Criminal Procedure — Reasonable-doubt instructions and structural error.

##### The sentencing remand does not prevent review of this completed conviction ruling

**Holding:** Under §1257 and Cox’s surviving-federal-question category, the separate sentencing remand does not prevent review of the finally resolved federal challenge to Sullivan’s conviction, which will remain whatever lawful sentence follows. Review does not reach the correctness of the penalty-phase relief or create a general exception for interlocutory criminal rulings.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support for this exact proposition; no Marks synthesis.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The completed federal conviction issue survives any lawful sentencing outcome; this does not create a general exception for interlocutory criminal rulings. Correctness of the separate penalty-phase relief is not reviewed.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the conviction-sustaining judgment and remand for proceedings permitting a new trial under a valid reasonable-doubt charge. No acquittal; prior penalty-phase relief survives and the original death sentence is not reinstated.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Cox Broadcasting Corp. v. Cohn, 420 U.S. 469, 480–481: surviving-federal-question finality applied to the distinct conviction and sentencing components.

### Antecedent questions of governing law

#### United States National Bank of Oregon v. Independent Insurance Agents of America, Inc., 508 U.S. 439 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 92-484 and 92-507.
**Decided:** June 7, 1993.

For the other question-level holdings in United States National Bank of Oregon v. Independent Insurance Agents of America, Inc., June 7, 1993, Nos. 92-484 and 92-507, see Business and Commercial Law — National bank insurance authority.

##### The court may decide a necessary antecedent question of governing law

**Holding:** In this live dispute, the court of appeals could consider whether the statute authorizing the challenged activity remained in force even though the parties initially assumed it did, after giving them a fair opportunity to brief the issue. This authority to identify governing law does not impose a general duty to raise every unargued issue or dispense with adversarial fairness.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The live dispute and opportunity for argument and supplemental briefing support consideration of the antecedent question. No universal duty to raise unargued issues or authority for advisory resolution follows.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the repeal holding and remand both consolidated petitions for the actual scope dispute.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Kamen v. Kemper Financial Services, Inc., 500 U.S. 90: a presented claim permits consideration of the governing legal arguments.
- Arcadia v. Ohio Power Co., 498 U.S. 73: a necessary antecedent question may be resolved.
- Swift & Co. v. Hocking Valley Railway Co., 243 U.S. 281: parties’ legal assumptions do not bind a court’s statement of governing law.

#### Hubbard v. United States

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-172.
**Decided:** May 15, 1995.

For the other questions in this decision, see Criminal Procedure — False statements and statutory court coverage (A federal court is not an included department or agency under this statute; Bramblett's incompatible all-branch construction is overruled; Reverse only the affirmance of Counts V–VII and remand to vacate them).

##### The court-entity question is properly before the Court

**Holding:** The Court reaches whether the federal court receiving the filings supporting Counts V–VII is a department or agency within offense-date §§6 and 1001. The governing-law inquiry concerns the same challenged convictions and the question presented; it does not authorize review of a separate judgment, enlarge review merely because merits briefs discuss an issue, or require decision of every unargued antecedent question.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas's Part I, all eight Associate Justices joining the complete analysis; Stone-Zsela joins only the conclusion that review is available.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- United States National Bank of Oregon v. Independent Insurance Agents of America, Inc., decided June 7, 1993: applied; necessary antecedent governing law may be considered in a live dispute with adversarial fairness, without a duty to raise every omitted issue.
- Yee v. City of Escondido, decided April 1, 1992: applied; new supporting arguments and independently presented questions remain distinct.
- Izumi Seimitsu Kogyo Kabushiki Kaisha v. U.S. Philips Corp., decided November 30, 1993: distinguished; Hubbard seeks review of the same three convictions, not an additional intervention judgment. Its prudential limit remains intact.
- Caspari v. Bohlen, decided February 23, 1994: preserved at its confined subsidiary-defense scope; it supplies no general briefing exception.
- Madsen v. Women's Health Center, Inc., decided June 30, 1994: preserved; its exceptional review required its own circumstances, and its nonreach limits are not displaced.
- United States v. X-Citement Video, Inc., decided November 29, 1994: its prevailing-respondent alternative-ground rule is not transferred to this petitioner seeking reversal.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The affirmance of Counts V–VII is reversed and remanded for lawful vacatur of those §1001 convictions. Counts I–IV and VIII–X remain undisturbed. No exact sentence, immediate release, general resentencing, retrial or new prosecution is ordered; implementation requires the actual criminal judgment and lawful proceedings.

### Antecedent grounds and constitutional avoidance

#### Zobrest v. Catalina Foothills School District, 509 U.S. 1 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-94.
**Decided:** June 18, 1993.

##### Antecedent legal grounds must be considered on this remand

**Holding:** The live reimbursement claim should return for adversarial consideration of its statutory and regulatory basis and any independently dispositive funding limits before this Court decides establishment. The lower courts must expressly decide whether to entertain the district’s forfeited defenses, considering the family’s opportunity to respond and prejudice; avoidance does not automatically revive abandoned grounds.

**Proposition-level authority:** O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court, Antecedent legal grounds must be considered on this remand portion; Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter (5 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Adickes, 398 U.S. 144 — ordinary preservation rule acknowledged, not bypassed silently.
- Singleton, 428 U.S. 106 — discretion exercised for this antecedent-law remand.
- Ashwander, 297 U.S. 288 — constitutional-necessity principle applied without an automatic forfeiture exception.
- Burlington, 471 U.S. 359 — statutory reimbursement context retained; no automatic award.

##### Lack of a duty and an affirmative prohibition are different

**Holding:** On remand, absence of a federal mandate to supply the interpreter cannot by itself establish that local provision or reimbursement is prohibited. The courts must determine the actual operation of IDEA, 34 C.F.R.§76.532(a)(1) and any properly entertained state-law defense, distinguishing a bar on religious worship, instruction or proselytization funding from a regulation implementing only the constitutional limit.

**Proposition-level authority:** O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court, Lack of a duty and an affirmative prohibition are different portion; Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter (5 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No Establishment Clause merits, reimbursement entitlement or revived Free Exercise claim is decided. A regulation implementing only the constitutional limit may not avoid the constitutional question; absence of a federal duty does not establish a prohibition on local provision.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate and remand for adversarial consideration of reimbursement authority and independent limits, including whether to entertain forfeited defenses and resulting prejudice; no award or permanent funding bar.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Burlington, 471 U.S. 359 — reimbursement authority distinguished from an automatic entitlement.
- Mueller, 463 U.S. 388; Witters, 474 U.S. 481; Meek, 421 U.S. 349; Wolman, 433 U.S. 229; Ball, 473 U.S. 373 — no merits treatment by the Court; competing aid precedents remain available.

### Temporal application of federal decisions

#### Harper v. Virginia Department of Taxation, 509 U.S. 86 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-794.
**Decided:** June 18, 1993.

##### An applied federal rule governs still-open direct review

**Holding:** When this Court applies a federal rule to the parties before it, that rule governs all still-open direct-review cases concerning events within its substantive scope, whether those events predate or postdate announcement. A State cannot use selective prospectivity to withhold the rule; Chevron Oil is displaced to the extent it permits that practice, without reopening final judgments or deciding every conceivable prospective disposition.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas’s Opinion of the Court, An applied federal rule governs still-open direct review portion; Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Davis, 489 U.S. 803 — nondiscrimination rule applied to pending claims.
- Griffith, 479 U.S. 314 — equal direct-review application extended to this civil setting.
- Beam, 501 U.S. 529 — its rejection of selective application clarified through a direct majority rule.
- Chevron Oil, 404 U.S. 97 — displaced only insofar as it permits selective prospectivity after application to the original litigants.

##### Retroactivity and the lawful tax remedy are separate

**Holding:** The state courts must apply Davis and determine the correction state law and federal due process require. If the State compelled payment before an adequate opportunity to contest the tax, meaningful backward-looking relief is required; lawful refunds, retroactive nondiscriminatory assessments or a combination may suffice, while a meaningful prepayment opportunity presents a different remedial setting.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas’s Opinion of the Court, Retroactivity and the lawful tax remedy are separate portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Final judgments are not reopened; pure prospectivity where application to the original litigants is reserved is not decided. Genuine procedural and remedial rules cannot reintroduce selective prospectivity. Virginia’s refund premise is not an independent state ground insulating the federal error.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand for application of Davis and lawful correction under state law and due process; no fixed refund amount, appropriation or assessment of absent taxpayers without lawful process.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- McKesson, 496 U.S. 18 — pay-first systems require meaningful backward-looking relief; alternatives preserved.
- Davis, 489 U.S. 803 — correction of unequal taxation distinguished from a particular refund formula.
- Beam, 501 U.S. 529 — procedural and remedial questions kept distinct from temporal governing law.

#### Reynoldsville Casket Co. v. Hyde

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-3.
**Decided:** May 15, 1995.

##### Reliance alone does not permit withholding a governing federal rule

**Holding:** A state court must apply a controlling federal constitutional rule to a case still open on direct review and cannot withhold that rule merely because a litigant relied on the invalidated statute. Independently valid procedural or remedial rules may determine relief, but cannot reproduce selective prospectivity under a different label.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg: seven direct votes; Kennedy and O'Connor concur in the judgment.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Finality, valid procedural bars, immunity, an independently justified equitable rule and a constitutional remedial requirement remain distinct possible grounds. Reliance must satisfy an actual lawful doctrine's prerequisites; this decision establishes none and does not guarantee survival of the tort claim.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Bendix Autolite, 486 U.S. 888: applies its invalidation of Ohio's interstate-commerce burden; no expansion of that constitutional holding.
- Harper, June 18, 1993: applies equal rule treatment on open direct review; final judgments and independently valid remedies remain distinct.
- Beam, 501 U.S. 529: applies rejection of selective retroactivity and preserves actual procedural/remedial questions.
- Chevron Oil, 404 U.S. 97: cannot support the selective temporal exception Harper displaces; no additional displacement is decided.
- Reich, December 6, 1994: distinguishes its apparent tax-remedy withdrawal; creates no automatic tort-claim preservation.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the Ohio Supreme Court and remand for application of Bendix without the invalid tolling provision. Independently valid defenses or remedies remain available; the Court makes no equitable-tolling finding and orders no final dismissal.

#### Federal Election Commission v. Legi-Tech, Inc.

**Docket or dockets:** D.C. Circuit Nos. 94-5379 and 95-5085.
**Decided:** February 18, 1997.

For the separate questions in Federal Election Commission v. Legi-Tech, Inc., D.C. Circuit Nos. 94-5379 and 95-5085 decided February 18, 1997, see Constitutional Structure — Cure of structurally defective executive action (Actual lawful reconsideration permits this enforcement action to continue).

##### Retroactive governing law and valid curative relief remain distinct

**Holding:** A governing structural rule applies to this still-open case, but its equal application does not require dismissal after an independently valid lawful decision supplies the authority needed to proceed. Reliance on the former arrangement alone would not justify withholding the rule or preserving defective authority.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of the Opinion of the Court (Thomas), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The original Commission is not treated as valid; continuation rests on the later lawful decision, not reliance on the former structure.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Harper requires equal application of governing law on open direct review. Reynoldsville Casket rejects reliance-only relief disguised as remedy while allowing an independently valid curative ground.

### Review of federal preclearance determinations

#### Shaw v. Reno, 509 U.S. 630 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-357.
**Decided:** June 28, 1993.

For the other question-level holdings in Shaw v. Reno, June 28, 1993, No. 92-357, see Election Law — Territorial racial design and constitutional deprivation.

##### Federal preclearance dismissal remains separate

**Holding:** The district court outside the District of Columbia cannot use this action to review the Attorney General’s preclearance determination under the Voting Rights Act; § 14(b)’s designated channel and Morris’s restriction on review leave dismissal of the federal defendants undisturbed. That disposition does not immunize the State’s plan from an otherwise cognizable constitutional or statutory challenge.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Preclearance is neither a command to maximize majority-minority districts nor a certificate of compliance with every law. The federal disposition does not immunize the State’s plan against an independently cognizable challenge.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Leave dismissal of the federal officials undisturbed, 9–0, under §14(b)’s designated channel and Morris’s nonreviewability rule; no alternative merits defense or order to approve another map is decided.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Morris v. Gressette, 432 U.S. 491: restriction on reviewing the Attorney General’s preclearance determination applied; independent challenges to the state enactment remain available under their own rules.
- Beer v. United States, 425 U.S. 130: nonretrogression distinguished from maximization or certification of compliance with every law.

### Scientific expert evidence

#### Daubert v. Merrell Dow Pharmaceuticals, 509 U.S. 579 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-102.
**Decided:** June 28, 1993.

##### The enacted Rules displace mandatory general acceptance

**Holding:** Frye’s mandatory general-acceptance prerequisite does not govern admission of scientific expert evidence in federal trials under the Federal Rules of Evidence. Acceptance remains potentially relevant under Rule 702, but its absence does not alone require exclusion and its presence does not dispense with the Rules’ other requirements.

**Proposition-level authority:** Blackmun’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Frye v. United States, 293 F.1013: displaced as a mandatory prerequisite in federal trials; not a Supreme Court Rule 702 holding being overruled.
- Beech Aircraft v. Rainey, 488 U.S. 153: statutory interpretation of enacted Rules applied.
- United States v. Abel, 469 U.S. 45: compatible common-law guidance retained, incompatible prerequisite rejected.
- Bourjaily v. United States, 483 U.S. 171: Rules supersede incompatible common law.

##### Reliable scientific foundation and fit require flexible preliminary screening

**Holding:** Under original Rules 104(a) and 702, the proponent must establish by a preponderance the preliminary foundation for scientifically reliable knowledge that fits the disputed factual question and will assist the factfinder. The judge examines principles, methodology and valid application using relevant nonexclusive considerations—testability and testing, peer review/publication, meaningful error information and operational standards, and acceptance—without making any one consideration an invariant prerequisite; admissibility does not determine ultimate causation or sufficiency.

**Proposition-level authority:** Blackmun’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The original Rules govern. No later Rule 702 text, universal technical-specialty rule, appellate-review standard or substantive causation finding is adopted. Cross-examination supplements screening for admissible evidence; Rules 403, 703, 706 and ordinary sufficiency rules remain available.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate the Frye-based summary judgment and remand for evaluation of material proffers and reconsideration of summary judgment. Further fair foundation submissions may be received; no direction to admit every expert.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Bourjaily v. United States, 483 U.S. 171, 175–176: preponderance for preliminary admissibility applied, not a substantive causation burden.
- Rock v. Arkansas, 483 U.S. 44: adversarial testing remains appropriate for admissible evidence; no excuse for unsupported foundation.
- United States v. Downing, 753 F.2d 1224: helpful fit and flexible reliability discussion informs interpretation, not a binding Supreme Court multifactor checklist.

**Later-authority backlinks:** Kumho Tire Co. v. Carmichael, No. 97-1709, March 23, 1999: extends original-Rule-702 reliability and fit screening to technical and other specialized knowledge, with case-specific flexible criteria and discretion over the reasonable screening method.

#### General Electric Co. v. Joiner

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-188.

**Decided:** 1997-12-15.

##### Admission and exclusion receive the same abuse-of-discretion review

**Holding:** Appellate courts review a trial court's admission or exclusion of scientific expert testimony for abuse of discretion. Legal error remains independently correctable, historical findings receive their proper protection, and permissible case-specific evidentiary judgments receive deference. The importance of an exclusion to summary judgment supplies no different or asymmetric standard.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's Part I, unanimous.

**Later-authority backlinks:** Kumho Tire Co. v. Carmichael, No. 97-1709, March 23, 1999: applies abuse-of-discretion review to both the reasonable reliability-screening method and the final technical-expert evidentiary decision.

##### Reliability includes the inferential connection to the offered proposition

**Holding:** Original Rules 104(a) and 702 permit the judge to examine whether the expert's reasoning reliably connects the scientific material to the conclusion actually offered. Credentials, accepted terminology, or an assertion of a weight-of-evidence method cannot substitute for an adequate connection; screening that connection does not itself decide ultimate causation.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's Part II, unanimous at the stated legal level.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Under the original Rules 104(a) and 702, the scientific foundation and fit must be established by a preponderance. Neither a study identical to the claimant’s circumstances nor independent proof from each study is required; animal work, nonsignificant associations, competing causes and individually inconclusive materials may support a reliable cumulative inference.

##### The PCB-only exclusion was permissible on this proffer

**Holding:** The described differences between the experimental materials and Joiner's exposure and disease, considered with the identified limitations of the human studies, permitted the District Court to find an insufficiently supported PCB-only inference.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's Part III, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; seven Justices. Stone-Zsela joins reversal without this application; Stevens would vacate for appellate reconsideration.

**Material application:** The experimental dose, subjects and tumor differences and limitations of the human studies permitted exclusion of this PCB-only promotion inference. Admission was not held necessarily forbidden; no finding that PCBs cannot promote lung cancer follows.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reversed and remanded, 8–1. The unchallenged furan/dioxin exposure ruling survives, and causation admissibility on that assumed exposure remains open. The full defense summary judgment is not reinstated; no mandatory expert appointment or new hearing follows.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

Daubert's flexible foundation, valid-application and preponderance rules are applied and supplemented by today's appellate-review rule; its distinction between admissibility and ultimate causation remains. Koon, Pierce and Cooter & Gell inform the allocation of legal correction and discretionary application. Ornelas is distinguished by its particular ultimate constitutional inquiry, not narrowed into a universal rule about mixed questions. Frye's mandatory general-acceptance prerequisite remains displaced. Rules 403, 703 and 706 and Rule 56 keep their separate functions. No precedent is overruled.

### Adequate and independent state grounds

#### Tennessee v. Middlebrooks

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-989.

**Decided:** 1993-12-13.

##### An independently sufficient Tennessee constitutional ground forecloses federal review

**Holding:** The Court lacks jurisdiction to review a state judgment when an adequate state constitutional ground independent of federal law requires the same judgment whatever the answer to the federal question. The Tennessee Supreme Court's original state constitutional analysis in Middlebrooks, expressly confirmed by its subsequent majority opinion in Howell, independently requires this resentencing judgment, so the writ is dismissed for want of jurisdiction.

**Proposition-level authority:** Justice O'Connor's Opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg; all nine Justices support the rule and its application. No combination of separate opinions is necessary.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Herb v. Pitcairn, 324 U.S. 117, 125–126 (1945): applied; jurisdiction does not extend to an advisory correction incapable of changing the state judgment.
- Michigan v. Long, 463 U.S. 1032, 1040–1042 (1983): applied to the clear, independently sufficient state basis; no new presumption or categorical rule about later state opinions is adopted.
- Coleman v. Thompson, 501 U.S. 722, 729 (1991): applied to the direct-review consequence of an adequate independent state ground; federal habeas exceptions are not decided or transferred into this case.
- State v. Middlebrooks, 840 S.W.2d 317, 341–347 (Tenn. 1992): its original state constitutional reasoning is recognized as independently supporting its judgment, without federal endorsement of its capital-sentencing analysis.
- State v. Howell, 868 S.W.2d 238, Part II.E and n.7 (Tenn. 1993): its majority explanation confirms the original state basis; its different facts, harmless-error result, and sentencing directions are not applied to Middlebrooks.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The original Tennessee constitutional analysis and Howell's express majority clarification together support independence. No universal rule about a later footnote, federal capital-sentencing holding, Cox finality ruling or transfer of federal-habeas exceptions is adopted.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The writ is dismissed for want of jurisdiction. The state judgment affirming the convictions, reversing death and ordering a new sentencing hearing remains undisturbed. The State may again seek death on a lawful basis; no federal vacatur, new remand, reinstatement of death or immediate life sentence is ordered.

#### Greene v. Georgia

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-5369.

**Decided:** December 16, 1996.

For the other questions in Greene v. Georgia, No. 96-5369, decided December 16, 1996, see Criminal Procedure - Capital-juror exclusions and state appellate review (Federal habeas deference does not bind Georgia's direct review).

##### The stated federal premise permits review

**Holding:** When the state judgment rests on a stated federal premise without a clearly independent sufficient state ground, the Court may review that federal premise. Georgia's treatment of Witt as controlling direct-review deference supplies that basis here; the Court's review does not prevent Georgia from independently choosing a lawful state review rule.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam Part I, joined by all eight Associates. Stone-Zsela agrees only to reach the question.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Long, Evans and Robinette's interwoven-ground rule is applied without enlargement or displacement of an actually independent sufficient state ground.

#### Ohio v. Robinette

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-891.

**Decided:** November 18, 1996.

For the other questions in Ohio v. Robinette, No. 95-891, decided November 18, 1996, see Criminal Procedure - Traffic stops and objective justification (Objective legality during and after a traffic stop; No indispensable federal release warning; voluntary consent and attenuation remain distinct; The remedy corrects the federal rules without deciding admissibility).

##### Review of the interwoven federal and state grounds

**Holding:** When a state judgment fairly appears primarily federal or interwoven with federal law and does not clearly state an independently sufficient state ground, the Court may review its federal basis. Ohio's intertwined constitutional explanation supplies no clear independent ground barring review here; Ohio remains free to supply greater protection on genuinely independent state-law grounds.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's Part I, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Breyer; six Justices.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Independent Ohio law may provide greater protection. The decision creates no exception to ordinary finality requirements.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Long and Evans are applied without changing the independent-state-ground rule or creating an exception to the ordinary final-judgment requirement.

### Airport fees and review boundaries

#### Northwest Airlines, Inc. v. County of Kent, 510 U.S. 355

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-97.

**Decided:** 1994-01-24.

For the other questions in Northwest Airlines, Inc. v. County of Kent, 1994-01-24, No. 92-97, see Constitutional Structure — The statutory airport-fee condition and its measure; Constitutional Structure — Allocation, capital charges and concession revenues on this record; Constitutional Structure — Unequal recovery percentages do not establish interstate discrimination here; Constitutional Structure — The independent constitutional challenge fails on the same tried record.

##### Jurisdiction, the assumed action and the unchallenged judgment

**Holding:** Federal-question jurisdiction under §1331 is distinct from whether the AHTA creates a private action; the Court assumes that nonjurisdictional action solely to reject the airlines' appealed claims without deciding that it exists. An alternative ground may defend the judgment, but the Airport's failure to cross-petition prevents using its no-action argument to eliminate the airlines' favorable crash, fire and rescue allocation remand; the separately unappealed parking correction also remains in force.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter; seven Justices adopt this threshold reasoning. Thomas independently accepts jurisdiction, the limited assumption and preservation of both corrections, but does not join the Court's statutory merits rationale.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Bell v. Hood and Burks v. Lasker: applied to distinguish federal jurisdiction from a statutory claim's existence; no hypothetical jurisdiction is exercised.
- Thigpen v. Roberts: applied to permit an alternative ground defending, rather than enlarging, the judgment.
- Trans World Airlines, Inc. v. Thurston: applied to the required cross-petition where relief would alter the adverse portion of the judgment.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No private AHTA action is established, and federal-question jurisdiction is exercised rather than assumed. AAIA private-action and surplus questions, primary jurisdiction and congressional displacement of independent constitutional review remain undecided. General aviation is not found predominantly interstate or intrastate. No double capital recovery or blanket reserve ceiling is authorized.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Sixth Circuit judgment is affirmed, 7–1 overall: the appealed statutory claim fails 8–0 in result on distinct grounds, and the constitutional claim fails 7–1. Blackmun does not participate. The unappealed parking recalculation to break even and the existing crash, fire and rescue allocation remand between airlines and general aviation are preserved without new merits endorsement. No additional remand, refund or mandate deadline is imposed.

### Appellate sanctions and preserved review

#### McKnight v. General Motors Corp., 511 U.S. 659

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-1113.

**Decided:** 1994-05-23.

For the other questions in McKnight v. General Motors Corp., 1994-05-23, No. 92-1113, see Civil Rights — Sanction relief leaves the underlying employment disposition undisturbed.

##### Preserving a substantial question for further review is not itself frivolous

**Holding:** If the only basis for sanctioning counsel was that controlling circuit precedent foreclosed an otherwise substantial, unresolved argument preserved for Supreme Court review, the sanction was improper: counsel had to take the appellate step to seek that review. The $500 sanction against attorney John S. Williamson, Jr., is vacated for reconsideration; an independent sanction ground requires its own lawful basis and actual support, and none is supplied by inference here.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam Opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; nine direct joins.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Mozee v. American Commercial Marine Services, Inc., 963 F.2d 929, and Luddington v. Indiana Bell Telephone Co., 966 F.2d 225: their circuit authority explains why the merits argument failed there, not why preserving a substantial request for higher review was frivolous.
- Rivers v. Roadway Express, Inc., April 26, 1994: its later temporal ruling does not retrospectively establish frivolity.
- Willy v. Coastal Corp., March 3, 1992: preserves the separate collateral character of a properly authorized sanction without deciding whether this appellate sanction was justified.
- Kokkonen v. Guardian Life Insurance Co. of America, May 16, 1994: preserves the need for a lawful source of judicial authority; no new appellate-sanction power is inferred.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Later resolution of the preserved merits question in Rivers does not retroactively establish frivolousness. The rule protects a substantial unresolved question properly preserved for higher review, not objectively baseless litigation or independently supported misconduct. No general construction of the circuit rule or Rule 60(b)(6) is adopted.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Only the $500 sanction against John S. Williamson, Jr., payable to the circuit clerk, is vacated and remanded for reconsideration. No independent abuse is found, no automatic sanction or reimbursement ordered, and the restored employment dismissal and separate Rule 60(b)(6) posture remain undisturbed.

### Civil statutory temporal reach

#### Landgraf v. USI Film Products, 511 U.S. 244

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-757.

**Decided:** 1994-04-26.

For the other questions in Landgraf v. USI Film Products, 1994-04-26, No. 92-757, see Civil Rights — Section 102 does not add its new damages to this preenactment conduct; Civil Rights — The jury election depends on an available statutory damages claim.

##### Determine statutory temporal reach before applying the presumption against new retrospective consequences

**Holding:** When Congress has prescribed a civil statute’s temporal reach, courts apply that direction subject to constitutional limits; absent sufficient direction, they ask whether applying the provision would impair rights possessed when a party acted, increase liability for past conduct, or impose new duties concerning completed transactions. If it would, the provision does not govern those events without clear congressional intent; a pending case or reference to earlier facts alone does not establish such an effect, and prospective relief, jurisdiction, procedure and collateral matters require examination of what the particular provision actually changes rather than an automatic reopening of completed acts.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens’s general-framework portion, joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Souter and Ginsburg; five Justices adopt this organizing rule. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas concur on a distinct relevant-activity approach, not as additional joins at this level of generality.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Kaiser Aluminum & Chemical Corp. v. Bonjorno, 494 U.S. 827 (1990): applied to make statutory temporal direction the starting inquiry.
- Bowen v. Georgetown University Hospital, 488 U.S. 204 (1988): applied to the presumption against retrospective legal burdens; no automatic civil constitutional prohibition is inferred.
- Bennett v. New Jersey, 470 U.S. 632 (1985): applied to protect substantive obligations fixed under earlier law.
- Bradley v. School Board of Richmond, 416 U.S. 696 (1974): retained in its collateral litigation-fee setting and reconciled with the new-consequence inquiry, not overruled.
- Harper v. Virginia Department of Taxation, 509 U.S. 86 (1993): distinguished as judicial retroactivity; its equal-application rule remains unchanged.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The rule is provision-specific, not a declaration that the whole 1991 Act is inapplicable in pending cases. Section 402(a)'s enactment-date clause, §109(c)'s later-conduct limitation for extraterritorial amendments, and §402(b)'s exception for disparate-impact complaints filed before March 1, 1975 with initial decisions after October 30, 1983 remain distinct. Judicial retroactivity under Harper does not control legislative reach. An express congressional direction concerning the temporal operation of a statute resolves the threshold statutory question, subject to applicable constitutional limits. Such a direction does not alone determine whether Congress may constitutionally require reconsideration of an already completed Article III judgment. The present case concerns statutory consequences for earlier conduct in litigation properly before the courts and does not decide that separate final-judgment issue.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Fifth Circuit judgment is affirmed, 8–1. No new damages trial, award or remand is ordered, and the existing equitable-remedy judgment and unreviewed constructive-discharge findings remain intact.

### Fee relief and prospective filing controls

#### Day v. Day, 510 U.S. 1

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 92-8788, 92-8792, 92-8888, 92-8905, 92-8906, 92-9018, 92-9101, and 93-5430.

**Decided:** 1993-10-12.

##### No new prospective noncriminal-certiorari condition on this record

**Holding:** The Court declines to impose a new requirement that Day pay the Rule 38 docketing fee and comply with Rule 33's printed-form requirements for unidentified future noncriminal certiorari petitions because the established record does not show that treatment of particular filings under existing rules is inadequate. This record-specific refusal neither requires a prior warning in every case nor makes prospective restrictions categorically unlawful, and it does not decide any current fee motion or displace existing authority over demonstrated abuse.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam opinion of the Court, supported throughout by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg; all nine participating Justices adopt the same rule, application, and limited disposition.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Martin v. District of Columbia Court of Appeals and Martin v. McDermott*, 506 U.S. 1 (1992), prospective-control portion: applies its record-specific refusal where treatment of particular filings has not been shown inadequate; neither a categorical prohibition nor a universal warning requirement follows.
- *Demos v. Storrie*, 507 U.S. 290 (1993), prospective-control portion: applies the same narrower treatment of unidentified future civil-certiorari petitions; no current fee outcome follows from that prospective rule.
- *Zatko v. California*, 502 U.S. 16 (1991): preserves discretionary denial on demonstrated current frivolousness and extreme abuse; its particular-submission remedy is not expanded into a future filing bar.
- *In re McDonald*, 489 U.S. 180 (1989): preserves its targeted extraordinary-writ authority and distinguishes that setting from the proposed future noncriminal-certiorari condition.
- *In re Sindram*, 498 U.S. 177 (1991): preserves its targeted extraordinary-writ holding without automatically extending it to these unidentified future civil-certiorari petitions.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Filing volume establishes neither frivolousness of a particular petition nor inadequacy of particular-filing controls. No constitutional right to unlimited fee-free discretionary review or categorical rule about prospective restrictions is decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The proposed new fee-and-form condition is declined. No current fee motion or underlying petition is adjudicated.

#### In re Sassower, 510 U.S. 4

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 92-8933, 92-8934, 92-9228, 93-5045, 93-5127, 93-5128, 93-5129, 93-5252, 93-5358, and 93-5596.

**Decided:** 1993-10-12.

##### No new prospective noncriminal-certiorari condition on this record

**Holding:** The Court declines to impose a new Rule 38 payment and Rule 33 printed-form condition on Sassower's unidentified future noncriminal certiorari petitions because the established record does not show that treatment of particular filings is inadequate. The refusal imposes no universal warning requirement, filing-count threshold, obligation to exhaust every conceivable narrower measure, or categorical prohibition of prospective controls, and it decides none of the current fee motions.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam opinion of the Court, supported throughout by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg; all nine participating Justices support this component's stated ground and disposition. Direct unanimous procedural authority; no underlying certiorari or extraordinary-writ vote is inferred.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Martin v. District of Columbia Court of Appeals and Martin v. McDermott*, 506 U.S. 1 (1992), prospective-control portion: applies its record-specific refusal of unsupported conditions on unidentified future civil-certiorari petitions; no categorical prohibition or universal warning prerequisite follows.
- *Demos v. Storrie*, 507 U.S. 290 (1993), prospective-control portion: applies the same individual-treatment approach without deciding any present petition's eligibility or merit.
- *Day v. Day*, prospective filing-control decision of October 12, 1993: applies its earlier same-day noncriminal-certiorari ruling within that ruling's actual scope; it supplies no finding about Sassower's papers.
- *Zatko v. California*, 502 U.S. 16 (1991): preserves discretionary responses to demonstrated current frivolousness and extreme abuse; its present-submission remedy is not expanded into a future restriction.

##### No new prospective noncriminal-extraordinary-writ condition on this record

**Holding:** The Court declines to impose a new Rule 38 payment and Rule 33 printed-form condition on Sassower's unidentified future noncriminal extraordinary-writ petitions because this established record does not demonstrate extraordinary-writ abuse and inadequacy of narrower treatment sufficient to justify the proposed scope. This refusal neither invalidates nor vacates existing restrictions, establishes that no other restriction exists, decides an underlying writ or current fee motion, nor weakens Rule 20, jurisdictional, criminal-proceeding, or habeas requirements; targeted controls remain available under their governing authority on a supported record.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam opinion of the Court, supported throughout by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg; all nine participating Justices support this component's stated ground and disposition. Direct unanimous procedural authority; no underlying certiorari or extraordinary-writ vote is inferred.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *In re McDonald*, 489 U.S. 180 (1989): preserves its targeted extraordinary-writ authority; its established abuse setting does not itself establish the predicate for this new condition against Sassower.
- *In re Sindram*, 498 U.S. 177 (1991): preserves its extraordinary-writ holding without mechanically imposing its remedy on this different established record.
- *Day v. Day*, prospective filing-control decision of October 12, 1993: distinguishes its civil-certiorari scope; Day supplies no extraordinary-writ holding that controls this separate question.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The civil-certiorari and extraordinary-writ questions have distinct grounds. Day's civil-certiorari holding does not itself decide extraordinary-writ control. Both prospective questions had nine participants; the seven-Justice participation in current motion No. 93-5252 supplies no different prospective coalition or current fee holding.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Both proposed new fee-and-form conditions are declined, separately. No current fee motion is adjudicated, and no existing restriction is vacated.

#### In re Anderson, 511 U.S. 364

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-8312.

**Decided:** 1994-05-02.

##### Financial permission follows a particular assessment of the present paper

**Holding:** Rule 39.8 permits denial of financial leave when the Court is satisfied that the particular submission is frivolous or malicious; prior unsuccessful filings do not by themselves supply that finding. Anderson’s affidavit supports inability to prepay, and the present petition’s specific, partly corroborated access-to-review contention supports granting this motion despite substantial independent obstacles to the requested extraordinary writ; the grant establishes no entitlement to that writ.

**Proposition-level authority:** The per curiam opinion and order, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; nine Justices grant the motion on this bounded ground.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Martin v. District of Columbia Court of Appeals: applied; the present-paper assessment preserves both its limited grant and its companion denial, rather than creating automatic leave for access claims.
- Demos v. Storrie: applied; an arguable, particular interference allegation can support fee permission without resolving the requested relief or default.
- Zatko v. California, 502 U.S. 16 (1991): preserved; discretionary denial on a demonstrated present-frivolousness and extreme-abuse record remains available.

##### This record does not justify a new condition on all future extraordinary-writ filings

**Holding:** A prospective extraordinary-writ fee-and-form restriction must rest on demonstrated applicant-specific abuse and a supported connection between that abuse, the future category restricted and the inadequacy of individual treatment. This record does not establish those predicates for the proposed new condition; the refusal imposes no numerical threshold, universal warning requirement, duty to exhaust every imaginable lesser measure, or categorical prohibition of properly supported targeted restrictions.

**Proposition-level authority:** The same per curiam opinion and order, joined by all nine participating Justices: Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- In re McDonald, 489 U.S. 180 (1989); In re Sindram, 498 U.S. 177 (1991); and In re Demos, 500 U.S. 16 (1991): preserved as authority for supported, targeted extraordinary-writ restrictions; no categorical displacement occurs.
- Day v. Day and In re Sassower, decided October 12, 1993: applied within their actual prospective-control scopes; neither supplies a disposition of its unresolved current fee motions or automatically governs all custody-review applications.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No access allegation is accepted as proved. Financial permission establishes neither inadequate D.C. Code §23-110 remedies nor habeas jurisdiction, exhaustion, default excuse, a hearing, response order or relief from a conviction. In re Demos (1991) and Demos v. Storrie (1993) retain distinct extraordinary-writ and present-paper scopes.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Financial leave is granted and the proposed new prospective condition declined, each unanimously. No payment-cure deadline applies; no existing valid restriction is vacated or enlarged. Financial leave adjudicates neither the extraordinary writ nor its independent Rule 20 and habeas prerequisites.

#### In re Maria Luisa Costell Gaydos

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-5831.

**Decided:** 1996-12-02.

For extraordinary and original relief in In re Maria Luisa Costell Gaydos, No. 96-5831, decided 1996-12-02, see Federal Courts — Extraordinary writs.

##### Financial leave and the complete current paper

**Holding:** Gaydos's current sworn financial showing supports leave to proceed without prepayment, and the separate, documented review-access contention in her complete paper defeats the proposed Rule 39.8 denial. Financial leave does not establish entitlement to extraordinary relief, dispense with ordinary form, service or timeliness rules, or determine the separate certiorari submission.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of the per curiam opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; nine Justices adopt this record-specific application.

**Material application:** The October 8 declaration reports no employment, $6,480 annual pension and alimony, approximately $300 in cash and accounts, a car worth about $200, and no dependents. Nothing in this record contradicts that present showing. Shieh requires assessment of actual means and permits particular clarification; it does not make affidavits irrebuttable. Martin, Jones and Shieh separately require assessment of the complete current paper. Although the demanded FOIA docketing has occurred and the original-jurisdiction request fails, the October supplement raises a different, arguable question: the applicant says ordinary review was obstructed by a demand for an additional opinion that does not exist. The February reasoned letter order and the July and September Clerk correspondence give that contention independent substance. Whether it warrants a writ is a different question. Rule 39.8 permits a supported denial; it does not compel denial merely because some requested relief is unavailable. No independent finding of malice supports denial here.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Rule 39.1 requires the motion to state whether leave was sought in another court and whether it was granted, together with the Form 4 notarized affidavit or section 1746 declaration. When a United States district court or court of appeals has appointed counsel under 18 U.S.C. §3006A or another applicable federal statute, no affidavit or declaration is required, but the motion must cite the appointment statute; no such appointment is established here. Neither misconduct nor timely correction of every deficiency is found.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The motion for leave to proceed without prepayment is granted.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Martin v. District of Columbia Court of Appeals and Martin v. McDermott (November 2, 1992): applies the distinct current-paper and prospective-condition inquiries; a defective request and filing history do not answer an independent arguable access contention.
- Jones v. ABC-TV (February 26, 1996): applies separate financial, present-content and future-category analysis; no financial default is inferred from incomplete historical descriptions.
- Shieh v. Kakita and companions (April 1, 1996): applies actual-means assessment, separate consideration of concrete access content, and the applicant/category/inadequacy requirement.
- Zatko v. California (November 4, 1991): preserves discretionary denial supported by particular-paper frivolousness or malice; no such complete-paper finding is made here.

##### Prospective fee and form conditions

**Holding:** No prospective condition issues for either noncriminal certiorari submissions or extraordinary-writ submissions. Such a condition requires a supported showing tied to this applicant, the relevant filing category, and the inadequacy of treating particular papers; the present record does not supply that showing for either category.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III-A of the per curiam opinion, addressing extraordinary-writ submissions, is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Part III-B, addressing noncriminal certiorari submissions, is joined by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; the Chief Justice agrees with its disposition without joining its reasoning. Each portion independently commands a majority.

**Material application:** Martin and Shieh preserve separate inquiries into a present paper and a future category. The actual current paper and the record before the Court do not establish a category-specific sequence showing that individual treatment will be inadequate. Filing totals or historical descriptions cannot supply the missing connection. Preserving a paid route and unrelated criminal access would narrow a restriction, but would not justify it. McDonald and Sindram retain authority for targeted protection against demonstrated extraordinary-writ abuse; neither establishes this applicant's predicates. The Court declines both proposed conditions on the present showing.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No universal warning requirement, numerical threshold, exhaustion of every conceivable lesser measure, or categorical prohibition of later supported restrictions is adopted.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Court declines prospective fee-and-form conditions in both identified categories. No paid-compliance deadline or prospective restriction issues.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Martin v. District of Columbia Court of Appeals and Martin v. McDermott (November 2, 1992): applies the distinct current-paper and prospective-condition inquiries; a defective request and filing history do not answer an independent arguable access contention.
- Shieh v. Kakita and companions (April 1, 1996): applies actual-means assessment, separate consideration of concrete access content, and the applicant/category/inadequacy requirement.
- In re McDonald, 489 U.S. 180, and In re Sindram, 498 U.S. 177: retain targeted process-protection authority on supported records; no new future restriction follows here.

#### In re Vey

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-8005.
**Decided:** April 14, 1997.

##### Whether the current motion establishes financial eligibility

**Holding:** Vey's compliant Rule 39.1 motion and proper affidavit or declaration establish present inability to prepay fees and costs. That financial determination does not establish writ entitlement or make every financial assertion conclusive.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam opinion, Part I, joined in full by all nine Justices; no separate procedural tally is announced.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Shieh* requires actual-means assessment and permits justified specific clarification. *Jones v. ABC-TV*'s unresolved particulars do not govern this established showing. *Gaydos* preserves the distinction between financial leave and examination of the petition.

##### Whether this complete petition warrants discretionary denial under Rule 39.8

**Holding:** Rule 39.8 permits denial upon particular-paper frivolousness or malice; unsuccessful earlier filings and failure to prove extraordinary-writ entitlement do not alone establish that predicate. Vey's specific record-access and reopening contention is arguable on this showing, with no independent malice established, so the Court declines denial and grants financial leave on the separately established inability to pay.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam opinion, Part II, unanimous in its published joins; no separate procedural tally is announced.

**Material application:** The alleged psychiatric-document conflict, reliance on one account without resolving it, and denied access present a concrete contention not conclusively answered by the available materials. General weakness in other allegations does not itself dispose of that contention.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No document falsification, withheld record, constitutional violation, or reopening entitlement is found. Access terminology alone creates no screening immunity, and Rule 39.8 remains discretionary when its predicate is supported.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Martin* supplies particular-paper treatment and preserves both its arguable-paper grant and supported companion denial. *Anderson*, *Jones*, *Shieh*, and *Gaydos* preserve distinct access-content analysis. *Denton* distinguishes clearly baseless allegations from ordinary disbelief without transferring the entire former §1915(d) regime. *Zatko* preserves supported discretionary denial.

##### Whether the record justifies a new condition on all future extraordinary-writ filings

**Holding:** A prospective fee-and-form restriction requires demonstrated applicant-specific abuse, a justified connection to the filing category, and support for the inadequacy of individual-paper treatment. This record lacks those predicates for a new restriction covering Vey's extraordinary writs, including criminal habeas petitions.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam opinion, Part III, joined in full by all nine Justices; no separate procedural tally is announced.

**Material application:** The mixed totals of eleven certiorari petitions, twelve extraordinary-relief petitions, and two bail applications over six and one-half years do not identify relevant repetition, contents, or why particular-paper treatment is inadequate. A retained paid route narrows a condition but does not establish its justification.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Grant present financial leave and decline the proposed new future condition; no existing valid restriction is rescinded. Financial leave resolves neither the original habeas petition nor the alternative mandamus or prohibition requests and grants no underlying relief, hearing, transfer, certificate ruling, or plenary review. No payment-cure date, default, or refiling duty is imposed.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No numerical abuse threshold, universal warning requirement, exhaustion of every conceivable lesser measure, or categorical immunity is adopted. Ordinary form, service, timeliness, jurisdictional, and substantive requirements survive.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Anderson* and *Sassower*'s distinct extraordinary-writ holding require the category-specific showing. *McDonald*, *Sindram*, and *In re Demos* preserve targeted controls on their supported records; another applicant's findings cannot establish Vey's abuse. *Felker* preserves original habeas authority without supplying entitlement to its exercise.

### Judicial disqualification

#### Liteky v. United States, 510 U.S. 540

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-6921.

**Decided:** 1994-03-07.

##### Section 455(a) applies the same objective appearance standard regardless of source

**Holding:** A federal judge must disqualify when an informed, detached observer, considering the relevant conduct and circumstances together, would reasonably question the judge's impartiality; a reasonable conclusion that a fair and impartial hearing is unlikely suffices, without proof of actual bias or impossibility of fair judgment. Judicial origin is relevant context, neither a bar to disqualification nor an added exceptional-intensity requirement, and outside origin is neither necessary nor sufficient; ordinary adjudicative views, adverse rulings, criticism and trial management do not alone establish improper partiality, while §455(b)'s particular limitations do not automatically narrow §455(a)'s independent appearance protection.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens and Souter. These five Justices adopt this rule at the same level of generality. Scalia, O'Connor, Thomas and Ginsburg agree with the judgment but do not join the ordinary-appearance formulation without their additional judicial-source requirement. No narrower-rationale aggregation is necessary.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Liljeberg v. Health Services Acquisition Corp., 486 U.S. 847 (1988): applied to objective appearance and the independent operation of §455(a); its distinct civil remedial inquiry is preserved.
- United States v. Grinnell Corp., 384 U.S. 563 (1966): its protection of legitimate adjudicative impressions remains effective; its personal-bias discussion does not impose a categorical source bar or mandatory impossibility threshold under §455(a).

##### The reported conduct, considered together, does not require disqualification

**Holding:** The identified rulings, restrictions, admonishments and other reported conduct in the 1983 and 1991 proceedings, evaluated cumulatively under the objective appearance standard, supply no reasonable basis to question this judge's impartiality. The recusal challenge therefore fails and the convictions remain undisturbed; this conclusion does not approve every underlying ruling or establish a harmless-error rule for an actual recusal violation.

**Proposition-level authority:** The application in Kennedy's opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens and Souter, supplies five votes under the controlling standard. Scalia, O'Connor, Thomas and Ginsburg separately reach the same affirmance under their judicial-source formulation; all nine support the judgment, without nine joins in the Court's full rationale.

**Material application:** The earlier trial's restrictions on political presentation, questions and answers, and closing argument are considered with the sentence petitioners regarded as excessive. The current trial's public opening admonishment, restrictions on events in El Salvador, and unspecified admonishments of codefendants are also considered. The further witness questions and alleged anti-defendant tone carry no additional specified hostile language. Testimony claimed relevant to state of mind is not found admissible merely because petitioners describe it that way. Refusing leave to appeal without prepaying fees, without a supplied reason or eligibility finding, establishes no retaliation. Refusal to address Bourgeois as Father, without a specified disparagement or discriminatory context, establishes no religious antagonism. No outside relationship, financial interest or extrajudicial information is established. These limits do not exempt judicial conduct from scrutiny; they identify what the reported circumstances actually support.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court does not decide §144 affidavit sufficiency or procedure, an independent §455(b)(1) actual-bias claim, the legal correctness of every evidentiary or sentencing ruling, an independent appeal-fee entitlement, a general kinship rule, or the constitutional structural-error consequences of an established biased adjudicator. No general automatic-retrial or harmless-error rule is adopted. The Court's consideration of context is not a new mandatory checklist or a presumption that all courtroom behavior is proper.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Grinnell: applied to the distinction between legitimate judicial evaluation and disqualifying prejudice, while examining this record under §455(a)'s objective standard.
