The unresolved title, cession, boundary, irrigable-acreage and water-quantity predicates require adjudication before any additional Fort Yuma water may be awarded.

##### II. The compensation compromise does not decide the reserved-water claim

**Holding:** Docket 320's consent judgment finally settles the United States' and Tribe's claims against one another within that docket's reciprocal release, but it neither actually nor by a clearly intended binding determination resolves the disputed title predicate and does not release the distinct reserved-water demand against competing users. The Master's asserted consent-judgment bar therefore fails, while the compromise's lawful claim-finality effect remains intact.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of Stevens's Opinion of the Court, unanimous. O'Connor and Thomas retain their different original-allocation ground opposing the Fort Yuma return.

**Material application:** The $15 million judgment compromises alternative invalid-cession, trespass and uncompensated-taking theories. The disposition of those alternatives does not establish that one particular title theory was adjudicated. International Building requires attention to what a compromise actually determined before assigning issue-preclusive force. The reciprocal release addresses claims between the United States and Tribe; its no-admission clause reinforces the absence of an intended title adjudication without nullifying the judgment or its final releases. The appeal waiver also stands.

**Operative remedy or transition:** O'Connor and Thomas's agreement on the separate Docket 320 and section 22 questions does not make them supporters of the Fort Yuma return: their original-allocation ground would independently end that enlarged water demand. The Court awards no additional Fort Yuma water, fixes no Fort Yuma boundary and resolves no disputed cession or title merits. Further original-jurisdiction proceedings, and implementing decree provisions faithfully incorporating the two approved agreements, remain necessary.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- **Arizona v. California, 373 U.S. 546 (1963), and the 1964 decree:** Preserve the original allocation. The Court does not decide the merits of the asserted bar arising from that allocation, and no Fort Yuma quantity is changed now.
- **Arizona v. California, 460 U.S. 605 (1983):** Applies its recognition of finality in the continuing original action while preserving its boundary-litigation reservation. Its omitted-acreage holding is not overruled or declared inapplicable to a timely Fort Yuma defense.
- **The 1979 and 1984 supplemental decrees:** Give effect to their conditional all-five-reservations adjustment language and its role in the litigation course; they do not themselves adjudicate the present preclusion merits or establish a final boundary.
- **United States v. International Building Co., 345 U.S. 502 (1953):** Applies its distinction between a compromise disposition and an issue actually determined; the ambiguous title predicate gains no issue-preclusive force from compromise alone.
- **Matsushita Electric Industrial Co. v. Epstein, February 27, 1996:** Preserves lawful consent-judgment effect and the separate binding prerequisites. Its section 1738 rules are not directly transposed to the federal Docket 320 judgment.
- **Baker v. General Motors Corp., January 13, 1998:** Applies the distinction between an instrument's actual adjudicative effect and an additional effect against distinct rights; its rendering-state inquiry supplies no duty to revive a forfeited defense.
- **Richards v. Jefferson County, 517 U.S. 793 (1996):** Preserves the need for a lawful basis to bind distinct rights; shared subject matter alone supplies neither claim identity nor binding scope.

For the distinct section 22 bar and the particular settlement terms, see Arizona v. California, No. 8, Original, June 19, 2000, under Federal Indian Law, reserved water and compensation judgments.

### Appellate finality of arbitration dispositions

#### Green Tree Financial Corp.-Alabama v. Randolph, 531 U.S. 79

**Docket or dockets:** No. 99-1235.
**Decided:** December 11, 2000.

##### Finality of an order compelling arbitration and dismissing every claim

**Holding:** An arbitration order accompanied by dismissal of all claims ends the action and is appealable as a final decision under 9 U.S.C. section 16(a)(3). A stay leaving claims pending is distinct; the finality holding does not decide whether dismissal rather than a stay was proper.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Stevens's Opinion of the Court, unanimous.

**Material application:** The district court dismissed every claim with prejudice, leaving no further judicial determination in that action. A possible later proceeding on an arbitral award is a separate action. The presence of substantive claims before dismissal supplies no exception to the statutory final-decision rule.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Appellate jurisdiction is affirmed. The separate cost-based enforceability disposition does not convert the completed dismissal into an interlocutory order.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Catlin v. United States supplies ordinary finality by reference to what remains for decision. Digital Equipment Corp. v. Desktop Direct concerns premature collateral appeals and does not preclude review of an actual final judgment.

For cost-based enforceability and the limited remand, see this case, No. 99-1235, December 11, 2000, under Business and Commercial Law, Effective financial access to arbitration.

### Certified campaign-finance constitutional questions

#### Mariani v. United States

**Docket or dockets:** Third Circuit No. 99-3875; judgment below, 212 F.3d 761 (en banc). No separate Supreme Court docket is supplied.
**Decided:** December 12, 2000.

##### Section 437h eligibility, standing, substantiality, and Supreme Court review

**Holding:** Section 437h's authorized-plaintiff requirement, Article III injury, and substantiality of each constitutional question are separate requirements. Mariani's uncontested presidential-voter eligibility, concrete stake in prosecution, and developed constitutional challenge satisfy them; review of the en banc judgment lies by certiorari under section 1254(1), not the repealed direct-appeal provision.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of O'Connor's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer; eight Justices. Stone-Zsela agrees only in reaching the questions.

**Material application:** Section 437h authorizes the Commission, a national party committee, or a presidential-eligible voter to bring an appropriate constitutional action in district court, with qualifying questions certified immediately to the court of appeals en banc. The developed false-source question receives an independent substantiality inquiry despite the district court's judicial-economy explanation. It is substantial even though it fails on the merits, and a threshold remand would identify no necessary further issue. The 1988 repeal's transition for then-pending Supreme Court cases and judgments entered before effectiveness does not apply here.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Party agreement and judicial economy cannot replace statutory eligibility, injury, or substantiality. The Fund Act's different procedure supplies no alternate route. The threshold decision adjudicates no criminal count and supplies no standing for every hypothetical recipient application.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** National Committee of the Reform Party of the United States of America v. Democratic National Committee, March 17, 1999, preserves independent substantiality and the distinct statutory tribunal routes.

For the corporate-source and independent false-source holdings, see this case, Third Circuit No. 99-3875, December 12, 2000, under Election Law, Corporate contributions and true-source attribution.

### Claim preclusion of federal diversity judgments

#### Semtek International Inc. v. Lockheed Martin Corp., 531 U.S. 497

**Docket or dockets:** No. 99-1551.
**Decided:** February 27, 2001.

##### I. What law governs the external claim-preclusive effect of a federal diversity limitations dismissal

**Holding:** Federal common law governs the claim-preclusive effect of a federal diversity judgment on state-law claims and ordinarily incorporates the preclusion law applied by the courts of the State in which the rendering federal court sits, except when that law conflicts with a specifically demonstrated federal interest. No such conflict exists in this California limitations dismissal, so Maryland must apply California's claim-preclusion law rather than an automatic nationwide rule.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Scalia), unanimous for both questions.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Section 1738 and the Full Faith and Credit Clause do not directly govern this federal judgment. General finality or uniformity does not establish a conflicting federal interest. Federal-question judgments, materially different process-sanction dismissals and the actual content of California preclusion law are not decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Maryland automatic-preclusion judgment is reversed and remanded to apply California claim-preclusion law through federal common law. The California federal judgment remains final; no refiling there, reopening or survival of the Maryland tort claims is assured.

##### II. Whether Rule 41(b)'s merits designation determines preclusion in every other forum

**Holding:** Rule 41(b)'s default merits operation governs the dismissal's rendering-court effect and does not itself dictate the claim-preclusive consequences in every other forum. The default yields when the dismissal order specifies otherwise and excludes dismissals for lack of jurisdiction, improper venue or failure to join a party under Rule 19; a merits designation under the default does not replace the federal rule stated in Holding I.

**Proposition-level authority:** The same unanimous opinion.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Rule retains its rendering-court function without supplying a nationwide preclusion code. The Court does not decide the distinct Rules Enabling Act question whether a federal court may bar refiling where a state court would dismiss without prejudice; direct validity of the California judgment is outside review.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Erie Railroad Co. v. Tompkins: applies the allocation protecting state-created rights from a removal-driven change in substantive consequences.
- Hanna v. Plumer: applies the concerns with forum selection and unequal administration while respecting the actual scope of the federal procedural rule.
- O'Melveny & Myers v. FDIC: preserves exceptional federal fields but rejects general uniformity as sufficient reason for displacement; the federal judgment supplies the source here.
- Atherton v. FDIC: applies the need for an identified federal conflict before substituting uniform substantive content.
- Baker v. General Motors Corp.: distinguishes lawful judgment effect from additional forum consequences; its section 1738 holding concerns state judgments and is not extended as a direct command for federal judgments.

### Departmental authority for federal criminal appeals

#### United States v. Hilario

**Docket or dockets:** No separate Supreme Court docket supplied; judgment below 218 F.3d 19.
**Decided:** March 19, 2001.

For United States v. Hilario, March 19, 2001, judgment below 218 F.3d 19, Constitutional Structure contains the duration, classification and judicial-appointment holdings; Federal Courts contains the independent appellate-authority holding.

##### Whether independent assistant and Department authority sustain the notice of appeal

**Holding:** An asserted defect in the United States Attorney's appointment does not invalidate this appeal where an independently appointed assistant and the Department's own litigation authority support the notice and appeal. The assistant's section 542 appointment, section 516's departmental authority, the Solicitor General's criminal-appeal authority and Rule 3(c)'s absence of a required personal United States Attorney signature defeat Hilario's notice objection independently of Gil's appointment validity.

**Proposition-level authority:** Breyer, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg join this Court holding. Stone-Zsela agrees with rejection of the notice objection without joining its ground.

**Material application:** The notice also bears an assistant's signature under an independent section 542 appointment. Department authority under section 516 and Solicitor General criminal-appeal authority under 28 C.F.R. 0.20(b) apply; Rule 3(c) does not require the United States Attorney's personal signature.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The appellate-authority conclusion supplies neither independent authorization of the indictment nor ratification of other prosecutorial acts.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The notice objection is rejected. The affirmed judgment permits prosecution under the appointment upheld in the Constitutional Structure entry.

### Immediate review of state decrees under Cox

#### Bush v. Gore

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-949; review of Florida Supreme Court No. SC00-2431.
**Decided:** December 12, 2000.

##### Reviewability of the operative contest decree under Cox's fourth category

**Holding:** Section 1257(a) permits review when the State's highest court has conclusively authorized relief despite a substantial federal objection that would foreclose it, later success on nonfederal grounds could eliminate review, and delay could seriously impair the asserted federal policy. Those conditions permit review of the operative December 8 contest decree, without authorizing review of hypothetical implementation or unfinished individual-ballot rulings.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Souter's Opinion of the Court, joined by every other Associate Justice; eight Justices. Stone-Zsela agrees only in taking review.

**Material application:** The preserved Article II objection would foreclose the commanded inclusion and examination, rather than merely change an evidentiary ruling. Nonfederal counting success could eliminate the need to seek review, and delay during presidential selection could impair vindication of the asserted allocation of authority. Unfinished counting and disputed figures do not defeat these predicates; urgency, consent, or lack of rehearing does not independently create jurisdiction. Earlier Article II, equality, and due-process presentation is not erased by denial of leave to assert the different blanket claim that section 102.168 never applies to presidential elections.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The blanket inapplicability claim and hypothetical implementation are outside the preserved review. Reviewability does not establish success on the federal objection.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Cox Broadcasting Corp. v. Cohn supplies its fourth, bounded category. Goodyear Atomic Corp. v. Miller distinguishes immediate review of an asserted complete federal bar from its ultimate merits.

For Article II, see this case, No. 00-949, December 12, 2000, under Constitutional Structure, Legislative direction of presidential-elector appointment. For equality, due process, timing, and remedy, see Election Law, Statewide judicial vote determinations.

### Review of state judgments and clarification remands

#### Bush v. Palm Beach County Canvassing Board, 531 U.S. 70

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-836.
**Decided:** December 4, 2000.

##### Review and clarification of unresolved legal dependencies in a final state certification judgment

**Holding:** A final state judgment may be vacated for authoritative clarification when unresolved dependence on particular state grounds prevents sound decision of the submitted federal challenge. The November 21 certification directive is final under section 1257(a), despite county implementation and a distinct potential contest, but its late-return restrictions, acceptance date, and federal-timing premise require defined clarification before resolution of the submitted Article II, section 5, and due-process merits.

**Proposition-level authority:** Parts I, II, and IV of the Court's per curiam opinion, joined by all nine Justices. Part III's separate due-process explanation has the eight Associate Justices' joins; Stone-Zsela agrees only in that component's remand disposition.

**Material application:** The state court had supplied substantial statutory reasoning, including the distinction between tabulation and the tabulating system, the later permissive late-return provision, and the existing manual process. The unresolved dependency concerns the further restriction on rejection to returns preventing a statutory contest or full federal participation, and the judicial acceptance date of November 26 at 5 p.m. if the office opened, otherwise November 27 at 9 a.m. It is not a finding that the state court provided no statutory reasoning.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The November 21 judgment is vacated and returned immediately for two clarifications: whether the restrictions and selected date independently implement pre-election legislation and what role constitutional power plays; and whether a particular federal electoral requirement, including section 5, was understood to compel the date or full-participation condition and how that affected the remedy. No federal jurisdiction is retained awaiting an answer. The order supplies no independent stay, recount prohibition, certification command, tally, winner, automatic reversal of administrative acts, or substantive ruling confirming the Secretary's authority.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No final Article II violation, due-process violation, or qualification for section 5's benefit is adjudicated. Article II does not categorically forbid ordinary state interpretation, while a conclusion that the remedial terms displaced legislative directions cannot be assumed. Equal protection, later contests, ballot standards, feasibility, elector totals, and the lawful winner are outside this case.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Minnesota v. National Tea supplies clarification of materially uncertain state grounds under sections 1257 and 2106. McPherson v. Blacker supplies Article II's legislative-direction setting without resolving whether this judgment construed or displaced it. Smiley v. Holm concerns congressional-election lawmaking and does not settle presidential appointment.

For federal timing and the separate fairness proposition, see Bush v. Palm Beach County Canvassing Board, No. 00-836, December 4, 2000, under Election Law, Presidential-election timing and fairness.

### Later-judgment review of an antecedent appellate ruling

#### Major League Baseball Players Association v. Garvey, 532 U.S. 504

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-1210.
**Decided:** May 14, 2001.

##### Whether a timely petition from the later judgment permits review of its necessary antecedent ruling

**Holding:** A timely petition seeking review of the later judgment in the same litigation allows review of an antecedent ruling necessary to that judgment. The earlier mandate binds the district court but does not shield its predicate from this Court's proper later review.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of the per curiam opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; eight direct joins. Stevens does not join and would obtain plenary consideration before deciding the merits.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Mercer and Hamilton-Brown Shoe supply the later-judgment review rule without dissolving the earlier mandate's effect below.

For Garvey's labor-arbitration review, independent alternative remedial holding and award-restoration mandate, see Labor and Employment, this case, May 14, 2001, No. 00-1210.

### Later review following a no-quorum statutory affirmance

#### United States v. Hatter, 532 U.S. 557

**Docket or dockets:** No. 99-1978.
**Decided:** May 21, 2001.

##### Whether the earlier no-quorum affirmance bars review of the later judgment's constitutional predicate

**Holding:** Proper review of the later judgment permits consideration of the antecedent constitutional liability issues despite the earlier section 2109 affirmance for want of a quorum. That affirmance retains its lawful force, including the mandate binding the trial court, but does not insulate those issues from this later review where the Court earlier could consider neither the merits nor whether to review them.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Part I, joined by Stone-Zsela, Scalia, Kennedy, Ginsburg and Breyer; six direct joins. Thomas accepts the Court's reach determination as the merits predicate without joining its rationale or casting a contrary threshold vote. Stevens and O'Connor do not participate.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The holding supplies no general authority to disregard unreasoned judgments, no general extraordinary-circumstances exception and no claim that every statutory affirmance lacks interparty effect.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Pink's distinction between judgment force and precedent remains, but an equal division after consideration differs from this inability to consider the case. Garvey Part I, Mercer and Hamilton-Brown Shoe provide the later-judgment channel; the separate no-quorum analysis answers the section 2109 objection. Durant, Hertz, Christianson and Messenger retain their own judgment and law-of-the-case boundaries.

For Hatter's Compensation Clause holdings, tax components, no-cure rule and remedial limits, see Constitutional Structure, this case, May 21, 2001, No. 99-1978.

### Review of settled federal issues in pending state prosecutions

#### Arkansas v. Sullivan, 532 U.S. 769

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-262.
**Decided:** May 29, 2001.

##### Whether the finally settled federal suppression ground is reviewable and permits departure from controlling federal law

**Holding:** The Court may review the finally settled federal issue underlying this suppression judgment despite the pending prosecution because that issue otherwise could evade review whether the prosecution ends in conviction or acquittal. A state court must follow this Court's controlling federal constitutional interpretation; additional protection must rest on an actual independent state-law ground rather than a different interpretation of the same federal rule.

**Proposition-level authority:** Unanimous reasoned per curiam opinion on the merits; no petition-stage tally is disclosed.

**Material application:** The judgment rested on federal pretext reasoning and an asserted authority to read the Federal Constitution more protectively. No independent state ground is invented to defeat review.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** New York v. Quarles supplies the analogous otherwise-evading-review route. Oregon v. Hass distinguishes independent state protections from the duty to follow controlling federal constitutional law.

For the distinct stop, custody, impoundment and inventory rules and the bounded vacatur, see Criminal Procedure - Traffic stops, added custody and inventories, Arkansas v. Sullivan, No. 00-262, decided May 29, 2001.

### Appellate notices and prompt signature correction

#### Becker v. Montgomery, 532 U.S. 757

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-6374.
**Decided:** May 29, 2001.

##### Whether a missing signature on an otherwise sufficient timely notice can be supplied after the appeal period

**Holding:** Civil Rule 11(a), applicable to this district-court filing through Appellate Rule 1(a)(2), permits an omitted signature on an otherwise sufficient timely notice of appeal to be supplied promptly after notification, including after the appeal period. The court must afford that prescribed correction opportunity before dismissing for the omission; correction completes the existing timely notice rather than commencing a late appeal.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's unanimous opinion of the Court.

**Material application:** Becker's notice timely identified the appellant, judgment and appellate court, and he promptly sought correction when first notified. He may sign the filed notice or supply a signed duplicate; his typed name alone did not sign this paper notice.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Timing and required identifying content remain mandatory, and the cure applies equally to represented litigants. Rule 3(c)(2)'s spouse-and-minor-children rule concerns persons already parties, yields to a clearly contrary notice and supplies no distinct correction deadline or adjudicated representation right. Authorized electronic signatures, underlying exhaustion and pleading issues remain outside review.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the jurisdictional dismissal, allow the prescribed prompt correction and restore the timely appeal for ordinary review. No general equitable extension or prisoner exception is created.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Smith v. Barry's timely-objective-notice distinction extends to this signature setting through Rule 11's express cure, without relaxing original timing or content requirements.

### Prisoner litigation and available administrative processes

#### Booth v. Churner, 532 U.S. 731

**Docket or dockets:** No. 99-1964.
**Decided:** May 29, 2001.

##### Whether the absence of administrative power to award damages excuses statutory exhaustion

**Holding:** Amended section 1997e(a) requires exhaustion of an actually accessible grievance process authorized to take responsive action on a covered prison-conditions complaint even when the process cannot award the money demanded. Limiting the prayer to damages does not remove that condition or create a compensation-based futility exception.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's unanimous opinion of the Court.

**Material application:** The process could investigate and respond, and Booth failed to complete its accessible review steps. The enacted removal of adequacy and effectiveness qualifications, rather than administrative usefulness alone, controls.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A nonexistent, wholly powerless or actually inaccessible process is outside this record; no comprehensive unavailability, procedural-default or limitations rule is adopted. Whether excessive force generally constitutes prison conditions is outside the accepted question. Section 1997e(c)(2) separately permits dismissal before exhaustion of a facially frivolous or malicious claim, a claim failing to state a claim, or one seeking money from an immune defendant; that permission creates no entitlement to unexhausted merits review.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm dismissal without prejudice. Completion of the actually available process and any subsequent timing questions remain subject to their lawful requirements; neither wrongdoing nor damages is established.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** McCarthy v. Madigan's statutory-primacy rule is applied; its damages-only result survives where no statute imposes exhaustion, while the PLRA supplies the previously absent command for covered actions. McNeil v. United States enforces an express administrative sequence without importing the FTCA's separate exceptions or timing rules.

### Prisoner injury restrictions and postfiling release

#### Harris v. Garner

**Docket or dockets:** Eleventh Circuit No. 98-8899; judgment below, 216 F.3d 970. No Supreme Court docket supplied.
**Decided:** May 29, 2001.

##### Whether release or supplementation removes section 1997e(e)'s restriction from an action commenced during confinement

**Holding:** Confinement when the action is brought determines section 1997e(e)'s application to claims for mental or emotional injury suffered in custody without the required prior physical-injury showing. Later release, even reflected in an otherwise adequate supplemental pleading, does not cure that defect in the existing action; a dismissal based solely on it must be without prejudice to an otherwise permissible post-release action.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy and Thomas.

**Material application:** Rule 15(d) permits supplementation on proper motion, reasonable notice and just terms, even for an originally defective pleading, but cannot alter the statute's selected commencement event. Neither ordinary efficiency nor exceptional expenditure of judicial resources supplies a release-based cure. The actual supplement's procedural adequacy is not decided.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The decision establishes no general rule for nominal, punitive, prospective or independent economic relief, no physical-injury threshold for other cases, no jurisdictional classification, and no constitutional-validity ruling. Limitations, tolling, preclusion and merits of a new action remain open; alleged misconduct is not adjudicated.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the en banc temporal ruling, preserving dismissal without prejudice of the six released plaintiffs' claims barred solely by section 1997e(e). Other requests are not newly dismissed and the remaining five plaintiffs receive no new relief.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** McNeil v. United States preserves an enacted precommencement condition without importing the entire FTCA scheme. Semtek International Inc. v. Lockheed Martin Corp. prevents procedural rules from altering substantive statutory consequences. Mathews v. Diaz's later benefits-application cure and separate exhaustion waiver do not control this historical commencement condition. Artuz v. Bennett requires provision-specific analysis; Randell v. Johnson's bounded loss-of-custody-review rule creates no release-alone PLRA exception.

### Judicial estoppel and accepted interstate positions

#### New Hampshire v. Maine, 532 U.S. 742

**Docket or dockets:** No. 130, Original.
**Decided:** May 29, 2001.

##### Whether a State may repudiate the boundary interpretation accepted as a basis for prior relief

**Holding:** A court may prevent a litigant from repudiating a clearly inconsistent position after judicial acceptance of the earlier position when the change would produce unfair advantage or impair the earlier adjudication's integrity. Inconsistency, actual acceptance and resulting advantage or detriment are nonexhaustive equitable considerations, not inflexible prerequisites; supported genuine mistake or material change in law, policy or essential fact can alter the inquiry.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's opinion, joined by all seven other participating Justices; Souter does not participate.

**Material application:** New Hampshire obtained judicial acceptance of the middle-main-channel interpretation of the same 1740 language it now invoked for a Maine-shore boundary. The offshore/inland remedial distinction does not reconcile those positions, and materials already available during the earlier proceedings establish no genuine mistake or intervening essential change. A State receives no exemption on these facts; negotiation or compromise alone would not establish judicial acceptance.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Ordinary governmental policy changes in other cases are not adjudicated. Claim and issue preclusion are unnecessary to decision.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Davis v. Wakelee supplies the successful-position principle; Cleveland v. Policy Management Systems Corp. requires actual inconsistency and contextual attention to explanations rather than categorical estoppel.

##### Whether dismissal establishes an enlarged inland boundary decree

**Holding:** Judicial estoppel bars the present shore-boundary complaint, but dismissal neither establishes inland coordinates nor independently adjudicates title. The 1977 marine-boundary decree remains effective only within its existing geographic terms.

**Proposition-level authority:** The same unanimous eight-Justice Kennedy opinion.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Maine's motion is granted and the complaint dismissed. No survey, affirmative geographic decree, title award, damages, injunction or further proceeding is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** New Hampshire v. Maine, 426 U.S. 363, supplies the accepted interpretive premise; the decree at 434 U.S. 1 retains its enacted marine geography without enlargement.

### Federal civil-rights claims in tribal tribunals

#### Nevada v. Hicks

**Docket or dockets:** No. 99-1994.
**Decided:** June 25, 2001.

##### Whether section 1983 authorizes the asserted tribal forum for these federal claims

**Holding:** Section 1983 does not itself confer state courts' general concurrent jurisdiction on tribal tribunals, and no adequate independent grant supports the asserted tribal forum for these federal claims. The §1983 claims must be dismissed from the tribal proceeding; further exhaustion cannot supply the missing forum authority, and their possible availability in a competent forum is not decided.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III of Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; seven direct votes. Stevens and Breyer would leave conditional cognizability for further inquiry.

**Material application:** A federal cause and the tribunal's competence require distinct authority. Section 1983's reference to an action at law, equity or another proper proceeding does not transfer state courts' general concurrent jurisdiction to tribal courts. Its separate judicial-officer injunction limitation permits relief only when a declaratory decree was violated or declaratory relief was unavailable; that different qualification neither protects these wardens nor creates tribal competence.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court does not decide the alleged federal violation, individual conduct, personal defenses or the availability and timeliness of a competent alternate forum. No blanket immunity arises from official employment.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The section 1983 claims must be dismissed from the tribal proceeding; exhaustion cannot repair the forum defect. Federal relief remains subject to its actual party, immunity and equitable requirements, without automatic injunctive or monetary relief.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Hafer v. Melo retains personal-capacity statutory personhood and liability subject to defenses, distinct from forum power. El Paso Natural Gas v. Neztsosie creates no general tribal removal mechanism or independent grant for these claims.

For tribal tort authority and the final no-authority exhaustion disposition in Nevada v. Hicks, No. 99-1994, decided June 25, 2001, see Federal Indian Law — Tribal tort authority over state enforcement searches.

### Finality of state judgments on substantive remand

#### Florida v. Thomas, 532 U.S. 774

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-391.
**Decided:** June 4, 2001.

##### State-judgment finality on an unresolved admissibility remand

**Holding:** A state judgment rejecting one search-incident theory is not final under 28 U.S.C. §1257(a) when a substantive remand may admit the evidence on another ground, effective later pretrial appellate review remains available, and none of the preserved practical-finality routes is established. The Court must dismiss this review without reaching the search merits.

**Proposition-level authority:** The signed opinion of the Court by Thomas, joined in full by the other eight participating Justices, directly controls the rule, application and dismissal.

**Material application:** Florida could still obtain admission under Chimel, and an adverse pretrial suppression ruling could receive effective appellate review before trial. The remand required substantive findings; proceedings were not effectively complete, the Belton issue might disappear, later review remained effective, and reversal would regulate admissibility rather than preclude prosecution. Ordinary delay established no distinct serious federal-policy threat.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The four Cox routes remain available when proceedings are practically complete, the federal issue necessarily survives, effective later review is unavailable whatever the outcome, or reversal would preclude the relevant litigation and delay seriously threatens federal policy. Ritchie's practical-finality qualification remains: a merely theoretical future appeal is insufficient. The Court does not decide Belton's recent-occupant rule or Chimel's application and creates no categorical ban on review of suppression rulings.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Dismissal for want of jurisdiction neither affirms, reverses nor vacates the state merits ruling. The existing Chimel remand remains operative without a federal admission or suppression direction.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Jefferson v. City of Tarrant: applies its distinction between a final judgment and settlement of an interlocutory federal issue; its practical qualifications remain intact.
- Cox Broadcasting Corp. v. Cohn: applies and preserves all four practical-finality routes, each tested separately.
- Pennsylvania v. Ritchie: preserves meaningful practical finality, including the inadequacy of a merely theoretical later appeal; it does not eliminate the substantive remand and effective pretrial review here.
- New York v. Belton and Chimel v. California: their constitutional merits receive no new interpretation or endorsement.

### Review discretion over a new operational defense

#### United States v. United Foods, Inc.

**Docket or dockets:** No. 00-276.
**Decided:** June 25, 2001.

##### Whether the Court must decide or develop the unpresented operational government-speech defense

**Holding:** The Court declines, as an exercise of review discretion, to decide the government's operational government-speech defense, which was neither raised nor addressed below. Its asserted inclusion within the preserved First Amendment controversy does not require the Court to decide a materially different attribution theory or order its development; the private-subsidy dispute is resolved on the ground properly adjudicated here.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter; six directly adopt Parts I–III.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The refusal is prudential, not a categorical forfeiture rule. Statutory appointment, approval and accounting controls exist, but the Court does not decide actual message attribution or hold that the Council can never speak for government. This nonpresentation is distinct from the commercial-speech argument, which the Court does not find waived.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirmance requires implementation of the private-subsidy judgment, not a new operational government-speech inquiry. The advertising holding and statutory implementation are recorded under First Amendment — Compelled advertising in an independent market.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Yee and Lebron preserve the difference between an included argument and a new question and the Court’s discretion to decline a late alternative. Lebron’s governmental-actor status does not determine every funded message’s attribution. Keller and Rosenberger distinguish institutional or funding status from the character of particular expression.

## Standards and Tests selection: Objective appearance of judicial impartiality

### Objective appearance of judicial impartiality under §455(a)

**Governing question:** When judicial or outside conduct requires federal judicial disqualification.

**Current rule:** A judge must disqualify when an informed, detached observer considering relevant conduct and circumstances together would reasonably question impartiality. A reasonable conclusion that a fair and impartial hearing is unlikely suffices, without proof of actual bias or impossibility of fair judgment. Judicial origin is relevant context, not a bar or exceptional-intensity requirement; outside origin is neither necessary nor sufficient. Ordinary adjudicative views, adverse rulings, criticism and trial management alone do not establish improper partiality. Section 455(b)’s particular limits do not automatically narrow §455(a)’s independent appearance protection.

**Authority by component:**

- Single objective appearance standard across sources: Liteky v. United States — Section 455(a) applies the same objective appearance standard regardless of source, 1994-03-07. Kennedy's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens and Souter. These five Justices adopt this rule at the same level of generality. Scalia, O'Connor, Thomas and Ginsburg agree with the judgment but do not join the ordinary-appearance formulation without their additional judicial-source requirement. No narrower-rationale aggregation is necessary.

**Present operation effective:** Liteky v. United States, 1994-03-07.

**Open question:** Section 144 procedure, independent §455(b)(1) claims and the remedy for an established violation remain unresolved. The four-Justice additional judicial-source threshold is not controlling.

**Holdings navigation:** Section 455(a) applies the same objective appearance standard regardless of source.

### Liteky v. United States, No. 92-6921

Merits decision, 1994-03-07

#### Chronology and Posture

Reported at 510 U.S. 540. Argued November 3, 1993. On writ of certiorari to the Eleventh Circuit, 973 F.2d 910 (1992), which affirmed the convictions and rejected recusal under a categorical judicial-source rule. The question is whether §455(a) can require recusal for conduct or statements during current or earlier judicial proceedings without an outside source. Petitioners seek relief from their convictions following denial of recusal; the United States seeks affirmance. The charges concerned willful destruction of federal property under 18 U.S.C. §1361 at Fort Benning. The earlier Bourgeois trial occurred in 1983; the prosecution under review was tried in 1991.

All nine participated at argument and decision: Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg.

#### Judgment

The Eleventh Circuit judgment is affirmed unanimously. Five Justices adopt the ordinary objective appearance standard; four concur in the judgment on a different statutory formulation.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Remedy or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Section 455(a) recusal challenge to the convictions | Affirmed, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | No Justice | Convictions undisturbed on this issue; no new trial, reassignment or remand |

The Court rejects the Eleventh Circuit's categorical judicial-source rule while affirming its judgment under the correct appearance standard.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Souter | Affirmance | Full opinion: objective appearance rule, statutory independence, cumulative application and limits |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | O'Connor, Thomas, Ginsburg | Affirmance | Judicial-source ordinary rule with objective deep-partiality exception; no join in the Court's opinion |

The Court's opinion has five votes. The concurrence has four. Their agreement that source alone is neither necessary nor sufficient does not erase their different broader rules.

#### Controlling Propositions and Authority

##### Section 455(a) applies the same objective appearance standard regardless of source

**Controlling proposition:** A federal judge must disqualify when an informed, detached observer, considering the relevant conduct and circumstances together, would reasonably question the judge's impartiality; a reasonable conclusion that a fair and impartial hearing is unlikely suffices, without proof of actual bias or impossibility of fair judgment. Judicial origin is relevant context, neither a bar to disqualification nor an added exceptional-intensity requirement, and outside origin is neither necessary nor sufficient; ordinary adjudicative views, adverse rulings, criticism and trial management do not alone establish improper partiality, while §455(b)'s particular limitations do not automatically narrow §455(a)'s independent appearance protection.

**Authority:** Kennedy's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens and Souter. These five Justices adopt this rule at the same level of generality. Scalia, O'Connor, Thomas and Ginsburg agree with the judgment but do not join the ordinary-appearance formulation without their additional judicial-source requirement. No narrower-rationale aggregation is necessary.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court gives effect to Congress's protection against a reasonable appearance of partiality. Liljeberg establishes that §455(a) protects public confidence independently of proof of actual bias and that a limitation in a particular §455(b) ground does not automatically govern the appearance inquiry. Grinnell explains why views properly formed from evidence ordinarily do not establish personal prejudice; its application does not make judicial origin a categorical shield under §455(a). Judges must assess evidence, make rulings and control proceedings. An informed observer understands those responsibilities and does not equate disappointment, impatience or criticism with partiality. But a judge's apparent commitment to favor one side cannot become acceptable merely because it arose during litigation. Requiring that fair judgment appear impossible would leave reasonable doubts unaddressed whenever some possibility of fairness remained. The Government's concern about tactical motions is answered by objective reasonableness and concrete context, not an extra statutory element. The Court decides the appearance claim presented, without resolving an independent actual-bias claim, affidavit procedure or the constitutional consequences of a biased tribunal.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Liljeberg v. Health Services Acquisition Corp., 486 U.S. 847 (1988): applied to objective appearance and the independent operation of §455(a); its distinct civil remedial inquiry is preserved.
- United States v. Grinnell Corp., 384 U.S. 563 (1966): its protection of legitimate adjudicative impressions remains effective; its personal-bias discussion does not impose a categorical source bar or mandatory impossibility threshold under §455(a).

##### The reported conduct, considered together, does not require disqualification

**Controlling proposition:** The identified rulings, restrictions, admonishments and other reported conduct in the 1983 and 1991 proceedings, evaluated cumulatively under the objective appearance standard, supply no reasonable basis to question this judge's impartiality. The recusal challenge therefore fails and the convictions remain undisturbed; this conclusion does not approve every underlying ruling or establish a harmless-error rule for an actual recusal violation.

**Authority:** The application in Kennedy's opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens and Souter, supplies five votes under the controlling standard. Scalia, O'Connor, Thomas and Ginsburg separately reach the same affirmance under their judicial-source formulation; all nine support the judgment, without nine joins in the Court's full rationale.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court examines the incidents together, rather than excluding them because they occurred in court. Restrictions on political speeches, cross-examination, witness responses and new facts in closing have ordinary trial-management functions. The judge allowed explanations of political purpose while limiting extended discussion of policy and events in El Salvador. Grinnell supports distinguishing such adjudicative work from an improper disposition toward a party. The additional witness questions, asserted tone, claimed state-of-mind exclusion, disputed sentence, fee denial and refusal of an honorific do not, on the circumstances identified, establish prejudgment or hostility that reasonably puts impartiality in doubt. Neither an allegation of error nor its repetition supplies missing hostile content. Although the lower court's categorical rule was incorrect, no specified unresolved factual predicate requires a remand before rejecting this recusal claim under the correct standard. Liljeberg separates finding a violation from selecting relief; here the first requirement fails. The Court consequently orders no retrial or reassignment and leaves independent challenges to particular rulings and constitutional remedies undecided.

**Application:** The earlier trial's restrictions on political presentation, questions and answers, and closing argument are considered with the sentence petitioners regarded as excessive. The current trial's public opening admonishment, restrictions on events in El Salvador, and unspecified admonishments of codefendants are also considered. The further witness questions and alleged anti-defendant tone carry no additional specified hostile language. Testimony claimed relevant to state of mind is not found admissible merely because petitioners describe it that way. Refusing leave to appeal without prepaying fees, without a supplied reason or eligibility finding, establishes no retaliation. Refusal to address Bourgeois as Father, without a specified disparagement or discriminatory context, establishes no religious antagonism. No outside relationship, financial interest or extrajudicial information is established. These limits do not exempt judicial conduct from scrutiny; they identify what the reported circumstances actually support.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Grinnell: applied to the distinction between legitimate judicial evaluation and disqualifying prejudice, while examining this record under §455(a)'s objective standard.
- Liljeberg: its separation of violation from remedy is preserved; its civil Rule 60(b)(6) procedure is not transplanted to this criminal direct appeal.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court does not decide §144 affidavit sufficiency or procedure, an independent §455(b)(1) actual-bias claim, the legal correctness of every evidentiary or sentencing ruling, an independent appeal-fee entitlement, a general kinship rule, or the constitutional structural-error consequences of an established biased adjudicator. No general automatic-retrial or harmless-error rule is adopted. The Court's consideration of context is not a new mandatory checklist or a presumption that all courtroom behavior is proper.

#### Precedent Treatment and Current-Law Effect

No earlier Supreme Court decision is overruled. The Eleventh Circuit's categorical source restriction is rejected; affirmation of its judgment does not preserve that restriction.

Effective March 7, 1994, §455(a)'s ordinary objective appearance inquiry governs judicial-source and outside-source allegations alike. Source remains relevant context, but no mandatory outside-origin or impossibility element applies. The Court adds this controlling clarification to the prior objective standard; it does not overrule Grinnell's result or Liljeberg. The separate writing's additional judicial-source requirement has four votes and is not current law. Gibson's panel-neutrality rule, Martin's particular fee determination and Weiss's military-impartiality rule retain their confined scopes; none supplies a categorical recusal rule for these facts.

#### Separate Positions Relevant to the Decision

Scalia concurs in the judgment, joined by O'Connor, Thomas and Ginsburg. They agree that an outside source is neither indispensable nor sufficient and that the Eleventh Circuit's categorical exclusion cannot stand. Their disagreement concerns the weight of judicial origin. In their view, attitudes formed from evidence or events in current or earlier proceedings require disqualification for bias or partiality only when they objectively reveal deep favoritism or antagonism making fair judgment appear impossible. The formulation concerns the appearance of the disposition, not proof of the judge's actual mental state. It applies to judicially generated attitudes; it is not a universal threshold for claims based on outside circumstances, which remain governed by reasonable appearance and still require improper partiality rather than origin alone.

Grinnell supplies their distinction between proper judicial assessment and bias. A favorable or unfavorable opinion is not improper merely because it is firm: it may reflect evidence that a judge must evaluate. Impropriety can arise because a disposition is undeserved, rests on knowledge the judge ought not possess, or is excessive in degree. The concurrence uses these categories to explain why source matters, without equating every outside view with disqualification. Ordinary judicial rulings are ordinarily subjects for appellate correction; surrounding statements or other circumstances may change what they reveal. Criticism, impatience, dissatisfaction, annoyance and anger within ordinary judicial limits do not establish partiality, while courtroom origin supplies no immunity for genuinely disqualifying conduct. Berger illustrates serious prejudgment without deciding an affidavit question here.

The four Justices read §455(a) alongside the personal-bias provisions while preserving its objective protection and its reach beyond subjective knowledge. They reject the Court's decision to dispense with the additional judicial-source requirement, but do not import every limitation of every §455(b) subsection into a new holding. On the complete reported allegations, they find ordinary rulings and administration rather than the deep partiality their formulation requires. They consider the honorific, tone, testimony and fee allegations without inferring religious animus, retaliatory reasons or unreported language from the bare allegations. Their affirmance does not endorse every trial ruling, and no remedial question following a proved violation is decided. Their proposed judicial-source requirement is noncontrolling; they join the judgment alone, not the Court's opinion.

#### Source Notes

The [official United States Reports account, 510 U.S. 540](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep510/usrep510540/usrep510540.pdf), 542–543 and 556 with note 3, supports the reported motions and allegations. The [Eleventh Circuit opinion, 973 F.2d 910](https://static.case.law/f2d/973/cases/0910-01.json), supplies the judgment and categorical rule below. The allegations remain allegations, rather than findings of religious hostility, retaliation, unlawful sentencing or improper evidentiary exclusion. The original trial motions and full transcripts are not independently available in these sources; the reported account does not specify further conduct. Section 455(a)'s text and its relation to the personal-bias provisions are set out in the official report. [Grinnell, 384 U.S. 563](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep384/usrep384563/usrep384563.pdf), and [Liljeberg, 486 U.S. 847](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep486/usrep486847/usrep486847.pdf), supply the earlier statutory authorities. No quotation of judicial language is necessary to this account.

#### Mandate, Remedy, and Stage

Supreme Court merits review is complete. The convictions remain undisturbed on the recusal issue, and the Court orders no retrial, reassignment or additional recusal procedure. No separate Supreme Court matter is retained.

**End of entry: Liteky v. United States, Merits decision, 1994-03-07.**

### California Dental Association v. Federal Trade Commission, No. 97-1625

Merits decision, May 24, 1999

#### Chronology and Posture

Argued January 13, 1999. On certiorari to the Ninth Circuit, 128 F.3d 720, which affirmed and enforced the Commission's cease-and-desist order. The questions are whether the FTC Act reaches this nonprofit association organized to benefit for-profit dentists and whether the actual price- and quality-advertising restrictions received sufficient rule-of-reason analysis.

The California Dental Association's (CDA's) roughly 75% statewide membership, commercial benefits and enforcement are described in the appellate record. The administrative law judge (ALJ) found practical restrictions on low-fee descriptions, broad discounts and quality claims without individualized truthfulness review. The Commission relied on per se treatment of price restrictions and alternatively on abbreviated analysis; the Ninth Circuit rejected the per se basis but sustained abbreviated review of price and quality separately. The ALJ and Commission differed on market power. Those positions are distinct from a Supreme Court factual finding.

All nine Justices participated at argument and decision.

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Remedy and scope |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| FTC statutory coverage | Sustained, 9–0 | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | — | Coverage is resolved; no final liability follows from it. |
| Price-advertising liability | Vacated and remanded, 5–4 | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas | Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg, Breyer | The existing abbreviated analysis is insufficient; further restraint-sensitive consideration is required. |
| Quality-advertising liability | Vacated and remanded, 5–4 | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas | Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg, Breyer | Same formal relief, with the distinct quality-information analysis below. |

The case returns through the Ninth Circuit. O'Connor, Scalia, Souter and Thomas would leave that court to decide whether it can assess the Commission's decision on the existing record or should return it for more extensive agency analysis and an enhanced record. Stone-Zsela would require the Commission itself to conduct the fuller inquiry. Neither institutional direction commands five votes. The common judgment finds neither final liability nor reasonableness and directs no damages or new sanction.

#### Opinion Topology

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court on statutory coverage | Souter | Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Unanimous coverage determination | Complete coverage rule and limits. |
| Opinion of the Court on competitive analysis | Souter | Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas | Supports vacatur of both advertising components | Flexible inquiry and both insufficiency applications; no final liability. |
| Lead opinion's remedial allocation discussion | Souter | O'Connor, Scalia, Thomas | Supports vacatur and further proceedings | Ninth Circuit choice between existing-record assessment and further Commission analysis; four Justices only. |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Stone-Zsela | No other Justice | Supports coverage and vacatur/remand | Joins coverage and competitive analysis; requires Commission inquiry instead of the four-Justice appellate choice. |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Breyer | Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg | Joins coverage; would affirm liability | Complete alternative rule-of-reason assessment of price, quality, justification and practical effect. |

#### Controlling Propositions and Authority

##### Coverage includes an association serving its members' commercial profit

**Holding and operative rule:** The FTC Act reaches a nonprofit professional association organized to carry on business for its members' profit when its activities confer proximate, substantial economic benefits on their for-profit operations. CDA satisfies that condition through its insurance, financing, commercial support and other member-business activities; nonprofit form, tax status and absence of distributions to the association do not eliminate the members'-profit branch of section 44.

**Authority:** Souter's coverage opinion, joined by all eight other Justices, directly controls this proposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The statutory alternative concerning members' profit would do little work if only the association's own earnings counted. CDA materially advances its members' commercial practices, so the outside suppliers' role does not make those benefits merely incidental. This application does not require exclusive devotion to member profit, a substantial percentage of total activity devoted to it, or proof that each member makes a net profit from membership. The connection must be real and proximate; professional education's presumed or incidental benefit alone is not held sufficient. The Court does not decide coverage of every charity, educational organization, nonprofit with an annual surplus, or commercial competitor without for-profit members. Ordinary statutory interpretation suffices, without deciding deference to the Commission's jurisdictional view. Section 45's sector exceptions remain; none is established for CDA. Neither professional status nor an unverified guideline's description of state law supplies immunity. Coverage authorizes inquiry, not condemnation.

##### The inquiry must test the restraint's actual competitive tendency and material explanation

**Holding and operative rule:** An abbreviated rule-of-reason inquiry may suffice when experience and the restraint's actual character make its likely anticompetitive tendency sufficiently clear to assess both the restraint and its material competing explanation. Where professional-information disparities make those effects insufficiently obvious, fuller analysis fitted to the restraint is required; neither a fixed sequence of labels nor a complete industry or market-definition study is invariably compulsory.

**Authority:** Souter's five-Justice competitive-analysis opinion controls this rule and both applications below.

**Controlling explanation:** NCAA permits inquiry calibrated to an established competitive mechanism. Professional Engineers rejects the claim that competition itself is undesirable; it does not dispense with examining a plausible claim that a practice improves reliable comparison. Indiana Federation of Dentists permits direct analysis of an agreement withholding a desired service, but does not make every professional-information restriction identical to that agreement. Khan and NYNEX require attention to the actual restraint and mechanism without converting this horizontal association into a vertical arrangement. The FTC's concrete enforcement findings are adverse evidence, not a substitute for the missing analysis of information quality. The Court corrects legal sufficiency and does not reweigh supported findings under section 45(c), independently affirm every economic conclusion, or decide whether substantial evidence supports ultimate liability. It finds neither restraint reasonable and creates no professional immunity. Legitimate restraints on materially false or misleading advertising remain possible under their actual conditions; no categorical per se issue is independently decided.

##### Price restrictions require more examination on this record

**Holding and operative rule:** The practical restrictions on fee descriptions and broad discount advertisements cannot be sustained on the abbreviated analysis employed here without adequately examining their asserted effect on the reliability of price comparison. Further inquiry must test the actual enforcement and claimed information benefits; the mere reduction of advertising does not settle the net competitive tendency.

**Authority:** The same five-Justice competitive-analysis opinion directly adopts this application.

**Controlling explanation:** Price communication ordinarily helps consumers compare and dentists compete. Bates recognizes that value while preserving legitimate deception controls. But an across-the-board discount without an intelligible baseline or scope may be unreliable, and a disclosure condition might improve comparison, obstruct it or do both. The reported restrictions on truthful descriptions and practically prohibitive disclosures remain serious evidence against CDA. Their existence alone does not adequately test whether these particular conditions improve reliable information before demanding empirical justification on the assumption of an already established harmful net tendency. This is no finding that every advertisement misleads, that every condition helps, or that CDA's explanation ultimately succeeds. The lower proceedings must perform the missing analysis without automatically adopting the ALJ's no-market-power conclusion, a local-share threshold or mandatory comprehensive market definition.

##### Quality advertising is not equivalent to service output

**Holding and operative rule:** Quality-advertising restrictions whose effects depend on the reliability and verifiability of professional information require fuller consideration than equating fewer advertisements with a restriction of dental-service output. The actual conditions and competitive explanation must be assessed; possible effects on demand do not themselves establish a producer-imposed service-output restriction.

**Authority:** The same five-Justice opinion directly controls this distinct application.

**Controlling explanation:** Restricting useful quality information can impede service rivalry, yet patients may have difficulty verifying professional claims. A restraint addressing unverifiable or misleading claims may improve information. Bates recognizes that quality-claim problem, while Indiana Federation's established desired-service restraint provides no automatic answer to this different mechanism. The court below could not settle the matter merely by translating fewer advertisements into reduced dental output. The Court requires neither measured output decline in every antitrust case nor acceptance of a blanket ban. Supported enforcement against truthful information remains material. Fuller examination may condemn either or both restrictions, but the present analysis does not resolve their competitive explanation. The discontinued fear-allaying policy is not treated as a continuing restraint.

#### Precedent Treatment

- NCAA v. Board of Regents supplies flexible inquiry fitted to established competitive tendencies; it creates no compulsory full-market procedure.
- National Society of Professional Engineers v. United States remains a bar to defending restraints on the premise that competition itself is undesirable; an information-quality explanation must be tested on its actual competitive terms.
- FTC v. Indiana Federation of Dentists is distinguished at the level of the restraint and information mechanism; its direct-effects method and separation of supported findings from legal sufficiency remain.
- Bates v. State Bar of Arizona supplies the value of truthful price information and the distinct difficulty of unverifiable professional quality claims.
- State Oil Co. v. Khan retains its vertical maximum-price holding and horizontal distinctions; its attention to competitive mechanisms informs this inquiry without conferring immunity.
- NYNEX Corp. v. Discon retains its vertical supplier-substitution holding and distinct horizontal categories; labels or improper purpose do not replace analysis of the actual mechanism.

#### Law After Decision

CDA falls within FTC coverage, but the existing price- and quality-advertising liability determination cannot stand on the analysis used. The required inquiry is proportionate to actual restraints and live competitive explanations. The Court establishes neither final liability nor professional immunity, and imposes no universal market-definition trial or required proof of measured service-output decline. Supported agency findings retain their statutory review status. No majority specifies whether the Ninth Circuit may complete the examination on the existing record or must direct Commission reconsideration.

#### Separate Positions Relevant to the Decision

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and in the judgment:** The Chief joins coverage and the holdings requiring fuller, restraint-sensitive inquiry. The suppression of truthful comparison and the possible value of substantiation or discount disclosure must both be tested. He would direct the Commission itself to conduct that examination rather than permit the Ninth Circuit to complete it without Commission reconsideration. He requires no full market-definition trial in every case and grants no immunity for restraints on truthful, nonmisleading information.

**Souter's remedial discussion, joined by O'Connor, Scalia and Thomas:** These four would leave the Ninth Circuit to decide whether it can adequately assess the Commission's decision on the existing record or should return it for more extensive analysis and an enhanced record. Correcting legal sufficiency neither authorizes judicial reweighing of supported facts nor mandates a new administrative hearing. They make no ultimate substantial-evidence determination.

**Breyer, joined by Stevens, Kennedy and Ginsburg, concurring in part and dissenting in part:** They join coverage and the flexible rule-of-reason method but find the existing analysis sufficient for both categories. Actual enforcement, rather than the Code's facial aspiration against deception, suppresses low-fee descriptions and practically excludes broad discounts. Truthful discounts can inform consumers without listing every undiscounted service price. The supported quality restraint suppresses useful information without examining whether particular claims mislead; the mechanism is interference with communicating qualities and matching dentists to patients, not a finding that the number of procedures fell. Verification problems do not justify the actual breadth once those effects are shown.

On that showing CDA bears the burden of establishing an offsetting competitive benefit. A merely possible advantage does not require the Commission to refute every conceivable theory. These Justices find the inquiry and record adequately answer the reliability claim. Membership, entry barriers, economic benefits and dentists' interest in remaining members support practical effect; the statewide figure is not a finding of equal shares in every locality. Indiana Federation supplies direct-effects analysis and respect for supported Commission findings, while NCAA calibrates depth and Professional Engineers requires a competitive justification. They would affirm the existing enforcement judgment without a new order, sanction or damages. They endorse neither every disclosure restriction nor all professional advertising, and find no need to decide the Commission's per se alternative.

#### Source Notes

The [Ninth Circuit opinion, 128 F.3d 720](https://static.case.law/f3d/128/html/0720-01.html) supplies the reported administrative findings, enforcement practices and competing analyses. The governing text is [15 U.S.C. §44](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-1994-title15/html/USCODE-1994-title15-chap2-subchapI-sec44.htm) and [§45](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-1994-title15/html/USCODE-1994-title15-chap2-subchapI-sec45.htm). The [official reported chronology, 526 U.S. 756](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/526bv.pdf), supplies the argument and decision dates. The reported account does not establish every term of the administrative order or every advertisement. No additional order term, exact local share, state-law command or new advertisement finding is decided.

#### Mandate, Remedy, and Stage

The case returns to the Ninth Circuit with coverage resolved and the enforcement judgment vacated. Further proceedings must respect the five-Justice insufficiency holdings and section 45(c)'s treatment of supported findings. The four-Justice appellate option and the Chief's mandatory Commission direction remain separately stated, without either becoming a majority command. No final finding of reasonableness, liability, damages, market power or new sanction is entered.

**End of entry: California Dental Association v. Federal Trade Commission, merits decision, May 24, 1999.**
